\widetilde{h}:\overline{\mathbb{D}}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}
be as in

Lemma
5.1.12
. Then the following functions are all complex CCP
functions:
\widetilde{h}
,
\overline{\widetilde{h}}
and

\widetilde{h}\,\cdot\,\overline{\widetilde{h}}=|\widetilde{h}|^{2}
.

Chapter 6
The real case: towards extending Krivine’s approach

6.1
Some facts about real multivariate Hermite polynomials

Another important estimation (even with upper bound
1
) which might help to
support our search for a “suitable” CCP function which is different from the
CCP function
\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin
in the real case, is included in the next
two results. We start with the real case first. To this end, recall e.g. from

[
21
, Chapter 1.3]
that for fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}

\{H_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}\}
is an ortho
normal
basis in
L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
, where the

k
-variate Hermite polynomial
H_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined
by

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}):=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}H_{\alpha_{i}}(x_{i})=\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{\sqrt{\alpha!}}\frac{D^{\alpha}\varphi_{k}(x)}{\varphi_{k}(x)}\,.

(6.1.1)

Here,
\varphi_{k}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}):=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\varphi(x_{i})
,

D^{\alpha}:=(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}})^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}})^{\alpha_{k}}
,

\alpha!:=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}!
and
|\alpha|:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}

(
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
y\in\mathbb{R},x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
), and

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{n}(y)&:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}(-1)^{n}\exp\big(\frac{y^{2}}{2}\big)\,\frac{\textup{d}^{n}}{\textup{d}y^{n}}\exp\big(-\frac{y^{2}}{2}\big)=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{\sqrt{n!}}\frac{\varphi^{(n)}(y)}{\varphi(y)}\\
&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\,\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor}H_{n,k}\,(-1)^{k}\,y^{n-2k}=(-1)^{n}H_{n}(-y).\end{split}

(6.1.2)

H_{n}
denotes the (probabilist’s version of the) one-dimensional Hermite
polynomial, where

H_{n,k}:=\binom{n}{2k}\,(2k-1)!!=\frac{n!}{2^{k}k!(n-2k)!}\,.

Thus,

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}\,\varphi_{k}=\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{\sqrt{\alpha!}}\,\frac{D^{\alpha}\varphi_{k}}{\varphi_{k}}\,.

(6.1.3)

Since
(y+i\,z)^{n}=\sum_{l=0}^{n}\binom{n}{l}\,i^{l}\,z^{l}\,y^{n-l}
and

\mathbb{E}[X^{m}]=\frac{(-1)^{m}+1}{2}\,(m-1)!!
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
y,z\in\mathbb{R}
and
X\sim N_{1}(0,1)
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}[(y+i\,X)^{n}]=\sum_{l=0}^{n}\binom{n}{l}\,i^{l}\,\mathbb{E}[X^{l}]\,y^{n-l}=\sum_{k=0}^{\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor}\binom{n}{2k}\,(-1)^{k}\,(2k-1)!!\,y^{n-2k}={\sqrt{n!}}\,H_{n}(y)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
X\sim N_{1}(0,1)
. In particular, we
reobtain the numbers

\displaystyle H_{2l}(0)=(-1)^{l}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2l)!}}\,(2l-1)!!=(-1)^{l}\sqrt{\frac{(2l-1)!!}{(2l)!!}}=(-1)^{l}\,\frac{\sqrt{(2l)!}}{2^{l}\,l!}\text{ and }H_{2l+1}(0)=0

(6.1.4)

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
(cf. also (
6.3.39
)). Recall
that the generating function of the one-dimensional Hermite polynomials is
given by (cf.
[
21
, (1.3.1)]
)

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\ni(\lambda,x)\mapsto\exp\big(\lambda x-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\big)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{\nu}}{\sqrt{\nu!}}\,H_{\nu}(x)

implying that in the
k
-dimensional case the equality

\displaystyle\exp\big(\lambda^{\top}\,x-\frac{1}{2}{\|\lambda\|}^{2}\big)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\exp\big(\lambda_{i}\,x_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{i}^{2}\big)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m!}}\,H_{m}(x)\lambda^{m}=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\big(\sum_{m\in C(\nu,k)}\frac{H_{\alpha}(x)}{\sqrt{m!}}\big)\lambda^{m}

(6.1.5)

holds for all
\lambda,x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
C(\nu,k):=\{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}:|m|=\nu\}
. Namely, since
|H_{m}(x)|\leq(2\pi)^{k/4}\exp\big(\tfrac{1}{4}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\big)
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
(see

[
38
, Lemma 1]
, respectively
[
78
, Proposition 2.3 (i)]
), the quotient
test implies that
\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu!}}\,H_{\nu}(x)\lambda^{\nu}
even
converges absolutely. Hence, each one of the
k
families

\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu!}}\,H_{\nu}(x_{i})\lambda_{i}^{\nu}\big)_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is
summable (
i\in[k]
). Consequently, it follows that also the family

\big(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{i}!}}\,H_{m_{i}}(x_{i})\lambda_{i}^{m_{i}}\big)_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}=\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m!}}\,H_{m}(x)\lambda^{m}\big)_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}
is summable.
(
6.1.5
) now follows from the reiteration of the double summation
principle and the associativity formula for summable families. Consequently,
we obtain (see also
[
21
, Lemma 1.3.2 (iii)]
):

\displaystyle\sqrt{m!}\,H_{m}(x)=D^{m}\exp(\lambda^{\top}\,x-\tfrac{1}{2}{\|\lambda\|}^{2})\big|_{\lambda=0}=\prod\limits_{\nu=1}^{k}(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{\nu}})^{m_{\nu}}\exp(\lambda_{\nu}x-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda_{\nu}^{2})\big|_{\lambda_{\nu}=0}

(6.1.6)

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

In fact, (
6.1.6
) allows a further
(purely analytic) proof of the Grothendieck
equality
and its
multi-dimensional generalisation. As a by-product, we provide a closed-form
analytical representation of the multvariate distribution function of a
Gaussian random vector
\textbf{X}\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho))

(cf.
Proposition
6.3.6
). In
general, closed-form analytical representations of general multivariate Gaussian
distribution functions are not available (cf., e.g.,
[
66
]
). All that
can be derived from the calculation of the following vital “Fourier-Hermite
coefficients”, which include
sign
as a particular case (cf.

Corollary
6.1.2
below):

Theorem 6.1.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
m=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
,

a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and

b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Put
I_{a}:=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}[a_{i},\infty)
and
J_{b}:=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}(-\infty,b_{i}]
.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{I_{a}}H_{m}\,\textup{d}\gamma_{k}=\langle 1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{I_{a}},H_{m}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}=0}}}^{k}(1-\Phi(a_{i}))\,\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}\not=0}}}^{k}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{i}}}\varphi(a_{i})H_{m_{i}-1}(a_{i})

(6.1.7)

and

\displaystyle\int_{J_{b}}H_{m}\,\textup{d}\gamma_{k}=\langle 1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{J_{b}},H_{m}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=(-1)^{c(k,m)}\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}=0}}}^{k}\Phi(b_{i})\,\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}\not=0}}}^{k}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{i}}}\varphi(b_{i})H_{m_{i}-1}(b_{i}),

(6.1.8)

where
c(k,m):=k-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{m_{i}\,0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,k\}

counts the number of non-zero components of the vector
m
.

Corollary 6.1.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
m=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
,

a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Put
I_{a}:=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}[a_{i},\infty)
and
J_{b}:=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}(-\infty,b_{i}]
. Let
\chi_{a}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow\{-1,1\}
and

\psi_{b}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow\{-1,1\}
be defined as

\chi_{a}(x):=2\,1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{I_{a}}(x)-1\text{ and }\psi_{b}(x):=1-2\,1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{J_{b}}(x)=-\chi_{-b}(-x).

\displaystyle\langle\chi_{a},H_{0}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=2\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}(1-\Phi(a_{i}))-1\text{ and }\langle\psi_{b},H_{0}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=1-2\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\Phi(b_{i}).

(6.1.9)

If
m\not=0
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}{}&\langle\chi_{a},H_{m}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=2\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}=0}}}^{k}(1-\Phi(a_{i}))\,\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}\not=0}}}^{k}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{i}}}\varphi(a_{i})H_{m_{i}-1}(a_{i})\\
&\text{and}\\
&\langle\psi_{b},H_{m}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=2(-1)^{c(k,m)+1}\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}=0}}}^{k}\Phi(b_{i})\,\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}\not=0}}}^{k}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{i}}}\varphi(b_{i})H_{m_{i}-1}(b_{i}),\end{split}

(6.1.10)

where
c(k,m):=k-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{m_{i}\,0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,k\}
. In
particular,

\displaystyle\langle\text{sign},H_{2n}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}=0\,\text{ and }\,\langle\text{sign},H_{2n+1}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}=(-1)^{n}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\frac{(2n-1)!!}{\sqrt{(2n+1)!}}=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{2^{n}}\,\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\frac{(n+1)!}{\sqrt{(2n+1)!}}\,C_{n}

(6.1.11)

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, where
C_{n}:=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}
denotes the

n
’th Catalan number.

Moreover, because of the fact that
H_{\nu}(-x)=(-1)^{\nu}H_{\nu}(x)

(respectively,
H_{\nu}(|x|)=({\text{sign}}(x))^{\nu}H_{\nu}(x)
) for all

\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, a
k
-fold multiplication of the values of
one-dimensional Hermite polynomials implies that

H_{n}(-x)=(-1)^{|n|}H_{n}(x)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\,.

Hence,

\displaystyle\langle g_{\rho},H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=\langle g,(H_{n})_{\rho}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=\rho^{|n|}\langle g,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}\text{ for all }g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\text{ and }\rho\in\{-1,1\}\,,

(6.1.12)

where
\mathbb{R}^{k}\ni y\mapsto f_{\rho}(y):=f({\text{sign}}(\rho)y)=f(\rho\,y)

satisfies
\|f_{\rho}\|_{\gamma_{k}}=\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}
for
f\in\{g,H_{n}\}
(which follows from a simple change of variables and the trivial
fact that
|\rho^{k}|=1
for all
\rho\in\{-1,1\}
). Obviously,
dependent on the smoothness structure of
g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
, it’s quite a
challenge to calculate the “Fourier-Hermite coefficients”

\langle g,(H_{n})_{\rho}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}g(x)H_{n}(x)\gamma_{k}(\textup{d}x)
explicitly. Regarding that particular problem, let us
note a first important fact (cf. also

Proposition
6.3.13
):

Proposition 6.1.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\textbf{X}\sim N_{k}(0,I_{k})
and
g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
. Then the function

\mathbb{R}^{k}\ni\lambda\mapsto\mathbb{E}[g(\textbf{X}+\lambda)]
is smooth around
0
. It
satisfies

\displaystyle D^{\alpha}\,\mathbb{E}[g(\textbf{X}+\lambda)]\big|_{\lambda=0}=\sqrt{\alpha!}\,\langle g,H_{\alpha}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=\sqrt{\alpha!}\,\mathbb{E}[g(\textbf{X})H_{\alpha}(\textbf{X})]\text{ for all }\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}\,.

(6.1.13)

In particular, if in addition
g
is smooth, then

\langle g,H_{\alpha}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=\mathbb{E}[g(\textbf{X})H_{\alpha}(\textbf{X})]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha!}}\,\mathbb{E}[D^{\alpha}\,g(\textbf{X})]

for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
.

6.2
Real CCP functions and covariances: a Fourier-Hermite analysis
approach

Fix
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k}),\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Put

C(\nu,k):=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}:|n|=\nu\}
and

\displaystyle p_{\nu}(f,g):=\sum_{n\in C(\nu,k)}\langle f,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}\,\langle g,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=p_{\nu}(g,f)\,.

(6.2.14)

Since the inequality of arithmetic and geometric means in
particular holds for any pair of elements, indexed by elements of the set of
finitely many elements
C(\nu,k)
, it allows (the proof of) a direct transfer
of Hölder’s inequality by means of summation over
C(\nu,k)
, whence

\displaystyle|p_{\nu}(f,g)|\leq\sqrt{p_{\nu}(f,f)}\sqrt{p_{\nu}(g,g)}\text{ for all }\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,.

(6.2.15)

Since
\{C(\nu,k):\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
obviously is a partition of the set
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
and

\{H_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}\}
is an orthonormal basis in
L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
,
Hölder’s inequality again implies that

\displaystyle[-1,1]\ni\rho\mapsto h_{f,g}(\rho):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}\langle f,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}\,\langle g,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}\,\rho^{|n|}=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}p_{\nu}(f,g)\,\rho^{\nu}

(6.2.16)

converges absolutely, and

\displaystyle{h_{f,g}(\rho)\leq\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}|p_{\nu}(f,g)|\leq\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}\|g\|_{\gamma_{k}}}\,.

(6.2.17)

Hence,
h_{f,g}
is well-defined and bounded. Note also that by construction

h_{f,g}=h_{g,f}
,
h_{f,g}=\frac{1}{4}(h_{f+g,f+g}-h_{f-g,f-g})
(due to
the polarisation equality) and
|h_{p,p}(\rho)|\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}\langle p,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}^{2}\,|\rho|^{|n|}\leq\|p\|_{\gamma_{k}}^{2}
for all
p\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
and
\rho\in[-1,1]
.
Consequently,
h_{f,g}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
.

Observe also that
for any

f=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}a_{n}H_{n}\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
and
any

g=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}b_{n}H_{n}\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
, it follows that
a_{n}=x_{n}(f):=\langle f,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}
and
b_{n}=x_{n}(g)=\langle g,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}
for all

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
, implying that
p_{\nu}(f,g)=\sum_{n\in C(\nu,k)}a_{n}b_{n}
,
whence

h_{f,g}(\rho)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\big(\sum_{n\in C(\nu,k)}a_{n}b_{n}\big)\,\rho^{\nu}\,\text{ for all }\,\rho\in[-1,1]

and
\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\big(\sum_{n\in C(\nu,k)}a_{n}^{2}\big)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}a_{n}^{2}=\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}^{2}
. In particular,

h_{f,f}(\rho)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\big(\sum_{n\in C(\nu,k)}a_{n}^{2}\big)\,\rho^{\nu}\,\text{ for all }\,\rho\in[-1,1]\,.

This immediately results in another important statement which should be
compared with
[
95
, Proposition 5 and Théorème 3]
and
(
6.4.49
):

Proposition 6.2.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f,g\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
. If
H
is a real Hilbert space,
then there exists a real Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
such that for any
u,v\in S_{H}
,

h_{f,g}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})=\langle\psi_{u}(f),\psi_{v}(g)\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\,,

where
\psi_{w}:L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}
is a mapping which satisfies

\psi_{w}(S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})})\subseteq S_{\mathbb{H}}
for any
w\in S_{H}
.
In particular, for any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, the following statements hold:

h_{f,g}[S]\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}\text{ for all }S\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}

and

h_{f,g}[A]\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\text{ for all }A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\,.

We will recognise soon that an
additional boundedness assumption

on
f=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}a_{n}H_{n}\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
itself is of utmost
importance in relation to an approximation of the smallest upper bound of

K_{G}^{\mathbb{F}}
(cf.
Theorem 6.4.9
,
Theorem 6.4.14

and
Theorem 7.3.3
). To perform this highly
non-trivial task, we have to look strongly for “suitable”
f=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}}a_{n}H_{n}\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
which in addition are bounded
(a.s.); i.e., we have to look for some
M>0
such that (pointwise!) for
(
\gamma_{k}
-almost) all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\displaystyle|f(x)|\leq M

(6.2.18)

(as is the case with (
6.3.40
)).

We are now fully prepared to extend these important facts to one of
our key results in this paper. In particular, we provide a multi-dimensional
generalisation of the one-dimensional case
k=1
(cf.

[
19
, Section 3.1]
) and specify a non-obvious
tightening of the upper bound of
h_{f,g}
. Here, it should be noted that the
inclusion of
Proposition
2.2.2
would allow to
view
Theorem 6.2.2
as a straightforward simple implication of
the key results in
[
114
, Chapter 11]
, including the consideration of

[
114
, Definition 11.10]
(cf. also
[
18
, Chapter 5.6.1]
,

[
130
, Lemma 2.2]
and
Remark
6.2.12
). However, we
give a self-contained proof, built on the well-established Ornstein-Uhlenbeck
semigroup (whose construction is recalled in the proof) and which sheds light
also on the impact of the negative correlation case
-1<\rho<0
in shape
of an alternating sign change in the related power series.

Theorem 6.2.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N},f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
,
\textbf{S}\sim N_{k}(0,I_{k})
,
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho))
. Then the following properties hold:

(i)

h_{f,g}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
,

\displaystyle h_{f,g}(0)=\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{S})]\,\mathbb{E}[g(\textbf{S})]\text{ and }h_{f,g}(\rho)=\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{X})g(\textbf{Y})]=\text{Cov}(f(\textbf{X}),g(\textbf{Y}))+h_{f,g}(0).

(6.2.19)

In particular,

h_{f,g}(1)=\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{S})g(\textbf{S})]=\langle f,g\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}
,

h_{f,f}(1)=\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}^{2}
and
h_{f,g}(-1)=\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{S})g(\textbf{-S})]=\langle f,g_{-1}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}
.

(ii)

h_{f,g}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is bounded and satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}|h_{f,g}(\rho)|&{\leq\big(h_{f,g}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(|\rho|)\leq\big(h_{f,g}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)}{\leq\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}\|g\|_{\gamma_{k}}}\end{split}

(6.2.20)

for all
\rho\in[-1,1]
.

(iii)

If
\rho\in(-1,1)
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}h_{f,g}(\rho)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{k}(1-\rho^{2})^{k/2}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}f(x)g(y)\exp\big(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}-2\rho\langle x,y\rangle}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\big)\textup{d}^{k}x\,\textup{d}^{k}y.\end{split}

(6.2.21)

(iv)

If
H
is a real separable Hilbert space and
u,v\in S_{H}
, there
exists a family
\{{\textbf{X}}_{w}:w\in S_{H}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued random
vectors, such that
{\text{vec}}({\textbf{X}}_{u},{\textbf{X}}_{v})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H}))

and

\displaystyle h_{f,g}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})=\mathbb{E}[f({\textbf{X}}_{u})g({\textbf{X}}_{v})].

(6.2.22)

(v)

If either
f
or
g
is odd, then
p_{2\nu}(f,g)=0
for all

\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and

{\text{cov}}(f(\textbf{X}),g(\textbf{Y}))=\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{X})\,g(\textbf{Y})]=h_{f,g}(\rho)=\rho\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}p_{2\nu+1}(f,g)\rho^{2\nu}\,.

In particular,
h_{f,g}
is odd.

Expressed in matrix notation,
Theorem 6.2.2
-
(iv)

leads directly to a further crucial result:

Corollary 6.2.3
.

Let
k,m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
f,g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
. Then the following matrix
representations hold:

(i)

For any
S\equiv(s_{ij})\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
there exist a
random vector
\textbf{P}_{f}=(f(\textbf{X}_{1}),\ldots,f(\textbf{X}_{m}))^{\top}

in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and a random vector
\textbf{Q}_{g}=(g(\textbf{Y}_{1}),\ldots,g(\textbf{Y}_{n}))^{\top}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such that
(\textbf{X}_{i},\textbf{Y}_{j})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(s_{ij}))
for all
(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]
and

\displaystyle h_{f,g}[S]=\mathbb{E}[{\textbf{P}}_{f}{\textbf{Q}}_{g}^{\top}]=\big(\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{X}_{i})g(\textbf{Y}_{j})]\big)_{ij}.

(6.2.23)

(ii)

For any correlation matrix
\Sigma\equiv(\sigma_{ij})\in C(n;\mathbb{R})
there exists a random vector
\textbf{R}_{f}=(f(\textbf{Z}_{1}),\ldots,f(\textbf{Z}_{n}))^{\top}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
, such that
(\textbf{Z}_{i},\textbf{Z}_{j})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\sigma_{ij}))
for all
(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]
and

\displaystyle h_{f,f}[\Sigma]=\mathbb{E}[\Theta_{f,f}]=\mathbb{E}[{\textbf{R}}_{f}{\textbf{R}}_{f}^{\top}]=\big(\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{Z}_{i})f(\textbf{Z}_{j})]\big)_{ij}\,,

(6.2.24)

where
\Theta_{f,f}:={\textbf{R}}_{f}{\textbf{R}}_{f}^{\top}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
is a
positive semidefinite random matrix of rank 1.

Corollary 6.2.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
,
r\in[-1,1]
,
\textbf{S}\sim N_{k}(0,I_{k})
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(r))
.
If
\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}=1
,
\|g\|_{\gamma_{k}}=1
and

\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{S})]=\mathbb{E}[g(\textbf{S})]=0
, then

h_{f,g}(r)=\rho(f(\textbf{X}),g(\textbf{Y}))

coincides with the Pearson correlation coefficient between the real random
variables
f(\textbf{X})
and
g(\textbf{Y})
.

Very recently, Krivine generalised the Grothendieck equality and constructed a
related function
\Phi_{F,G}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow\{-1,1\}
in the real case
(cf.
[
96
]
). In fact, if
\varepsilon>0
is fixed,

Proposition
2.2.2
implies that

\Phi_{F,G}=h_{f,g}
, where

\mathbb{R}^{3}\ni(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}\mapsto f(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}):=\text{sign}\Big(\big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\sqrt{2}\varepsilon\,H_{2}(x_{0}))\\
\sin(\sqrt{2}\varepsilon\,H_{2}(x_{0}))\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{2}}\Big)=\text{sign}(F(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}))

and

\mathbb{R}^{3}\ni(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})^{\top}\mapsto g(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2}):=\text{sign}\Big(\big\langle\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\sqrt{2}\varepsilon\,H_{2}(y_{0}))\\
-\sin(\sqrt{2}\varepsilon\,H_{2}(y_{0}))\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{2}}\Big)=\text{sign}(G(y_{0},y_{1},y_{2})).

The function
\Phi_{F,G}
in
[
96
]
namely proves to be a beautiful
example that fulfils the following statement (if
k=1
and
n=2
):

Proposition 6.2.5
.

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X}_{0},\textbf{X},\textbf{Y}_{0},\textbf{Y})
be a random vector, which maps into
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{2(k+n)}
, such that

{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X}_{0},\textbf{X},\textbf{Y}_{0},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2(k+n)}(0,\Sigma_{2(k+n)}(\rho))
. Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
G:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two
arbitrary measurable functions. Put
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni(x_{0},x)\mapsto f(x_{0},x):=\text{sign}(\langle F(x_{0}),x\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n}})
and

\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni(y_{0},y)\mapsto g(y_{0},y):=\text{sign}(\langle G(y_{0}),y\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n}})
. Then

\displaystyle h_{f,g}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\arcsin\big(\rho\,\langle F(\textbf{X}_{0}),G(\textbf{Y}_{0})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n}}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\arcsin\big(\rho\,\langle F(x_{0}),G(y_{0})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n}}\big)\,\exp\big(-\frac{\|x_{0}\|^{2}+\|y_{0}\|^{2}-2\rho\langle x_{0},y_{0}\rangle}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\big)\frac{\textup{d}^{k}x_{0}\,\textup{d}^{k}y_{0}}{(2\pi)^{k}(1-\rho^{2})^{k/2}}.

In case of
f=g
some important extra analytical facts emerge;
particularly if in addition
f
is odd (which is the relevant case for the topic
of this paper):

Theorem 6.2.6
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
be odd, such that

r:=\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}^{2}>0
. Then the following properties hold:

(i)

h_{f,f}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
.

(ii)

h_{f,f}
is a CCP function if and only if

r=1
.

(iii)

h_{f,f}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-r,r]
is an odd strictly
increasing homeomorphism, which satisfies
h_{f,f}((-1,0))=(-r,0)
and

h_{f,f}((0,1))=(0,r)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle 0\leq h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}f(x)x_{i}\gamma_{k}(\textup{d}x)\big)^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2k}}f(x)f(y)\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}\gamma_{2k}(\textup{d}(x,y)).

(6.2.25)

(iv)

If
h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)>0
, then
h_{f,f}^{\prime}(\rho)>0
for
all
\rho\in(-1,1)
. In particular,
h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}=\big(h_{f,f}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)^{-1}
is real analytic on
(-r,r)

if and only if
h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)>0
.

(v)

If
h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
, then

\displaystyle\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(y)\geq\frac{1}{h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)}\,y\text{ for all }y\in[0,r]\,.

(6.2.26)

In particular,
s(r,f):=\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r)>0
. If
\alpha>0
and
h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)<\frac{r}{\alpha}
,
then
s(r,f)>\alpha
.
Moreover,

[-1,1]\ni t\mapsto\psi(t):=\frac{1}{s(r,f)}\,\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r\,t)

is an odd, strictly increasing and homeomorphic CCP function. In particular, the
function
\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}:[-r,r]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}[-s(r,f),s(r,f)]
is strictly increasing, odd and
homeomorphic, as well as its inverse.
\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(y)=s(r,f)\,\psi(\frac{y}{r})
for all
y\in[-r,r]

and
\big(\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}\big)^{-1}(x)=r\,\psi^{-1}(\frac{x}{s(r,f)})\,\text{ for all }\,x\in[-s(r,f),s(r,f)]
.

A first implication for
odd
CCP functions is a strong
improvement of the boundedness condition in
Theorem 6.2.2
-
(ii)
;
induced by the Schwarz lemma from complex analysis:

Proposition 6.2.7
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
be odd. Assume that
h_{f,f}(r)\not=r
for some
r\in(0,1)
. Then

\displaystyle h_{f,f}(\rho)<\rho\,\text{ for all }\,\rho\in(0,1)\,\text{ and }\,h_{f,f}(\tau)>\tau\,\text{ for all }\,\tau\in(-1,0).

(6.2.27)

Moreover,
0\leq h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)<1
. If in addition,
h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
, then

\displaystyle\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)>1\,.

(6.2.28)

All of a sudden we end up with an important general and non-obvious
structural statement about
odd
CCP functions; namely:

Corollary 6.2.8
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
be odd. Assume that
h_{f,f}(r)\not=r
for some
r\in(0,1)
. Then
h_{f,f}^{-1}
is not a CCP function.

We will recognise soon that
Theorem 6.2.6
is
strongly linked with the value of the Grothendieck constant
K_{G}^{\mathbb{F}}
(cf. Theorem

6.4.14
). We namely obtain in a natural way another
significant new definition; the so-called “hyperbolic CCP transform”. To make
this concept understandable, recall
Lemma
5.1.12

and
Remark
5.1.14
and reconsider the odd CCP
function
\psi:=h_{\text{sign},\text{sign}}=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin
; i.e.,
the Grothendieck function. Since
\psi^{-1}=\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}\,\cdot)
on

[-1,1]
, it follows that
\big(\psi^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(\tau)=\sinh(\frac{\pi}{2}\tau)=\frac{1}{i}\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}\,i\tau)
for
all
\tau\in[-1,1]
. Note that
\big(\psi^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)=\sinh(\pi/2)>1
, implying that
[-1,1]\subseteq(-\sinh(\pi/2),\sinh(\pi/2))
. Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{2}{\pi}\,\ln(y+\sqrt{y^{2}+1})&=\frac{2}{\pi}\sinh^{-1}(y)=\big(\big(\psi^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}\big)^{-1}(y)\\
&=\frac{2}{\pi}\big(\frac{1}{i}\,\sin^{-1}(i\,y)\big)=\frac{1}{i}\,\widetilde{\psi}(iy)\end{split}

(6.2.29)

for all
y\in[-1,1]
. The Maclaurin series representation of the real CCP
function
\psi
therefore implies the Maclaurin series of
y\mapsto\frac{1}{i}\,\widetilde{\psi}(iy)
on (the whole of)
\mathbb{R}
is given by

\frac{1}{i}\,\widetilde{\psi}(iy)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Gauss_Copula_special_case_arcsin}}}{{=}}\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{\nu}\,\frac{((2\nu-1)!!)^{2}}{(2\nu+1)!}y^{2\nu+1}=-\frac{1}{3\pi}+\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu=0}}{{\nu\not=1}}}^{\infty}(-1)^{\nu}\,\frac{((2\nu-1)!!)^{2}}{(2\nu+1)!}y^{2\nu+1}\,.

Consequently, it follows that

\big(\big(\psi^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}\big)^{-1}(\rho)=\frac{1}{i}\,\widetilde{\psi}(i\rho)<\psi(\rho)<\rho\,\text{ for all }\,\rho\in(0,1)\,.

Theorem 6.2.6
and

Proposition
6.2.7
, together with

Remark
5.1.14
imply that the Grothendieck
function is a special case of

Lemma 6.2.9

(
Hyperbolic CCP transform
)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\psi=h_{f,f}
, where
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
is odd.
Assume that
\psi^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
and
\psi(r)\not=r
for some
r\in(0,1)
. Let the complex function
F:=

\mathrel{\overset{\scalebox{4.2}[1]{$\sim$}}{\phantom{\psi{|}_{(-1,1)}}}}

\psi{|}_{(-1,1)}

:\overline{\mathbb{D}}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}
be defined as in
Lemma
5.1.12
. Then

F(\mathbb{D})\subseteq\mathbb{D}
and

F(z)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}p_{2\nu+1}(f,f)\,z^{2\nu+1}\,\text{ for all }\,z\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}\,,

where each
p_{2\nu+1}(f,f)\in[0,\infty)
satisfies
(
6.2.14
). Put
s^{\ast}:=(\psi^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)})_{\text{abs}}(1)
. Then
s^{\ast}>1
and

\psi^{\text{hyp}}:=((\psi^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)})_{\text{abs}})^{-1}:[-s^{\ast},s^{\ast}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}[-1,1]

is an odd, strictly increasing homeomorphism, which satisfies
[-1,1]\subseteq(-s^{\ast},s^{\ast})
,
\psi^{\text{hyp}}((0,1])\subseteq(0,1)
and

\psi^{\text{hyp}}([-1,0))\subseteq(-1,0)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle 0<\psi^{\text{hyp}}(1)\leq\psi^{\prime}(0)<1\,,

(6.2.30)

and the following two statements hold:

(i)

|\psi^{\text{hyp}}(\rho)|<\psi(|\rho|)<|\rho|\,\text{ for all }\,\rho\in(-1,1)\setminus\{0\}\,.

(ii)

If
\text{sign}((\psi^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)})^{(2n+1)}(0))=(-1)^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

\psi^{\text{hyp}}(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{\nu}\,p_{2\nu+1}(f,f)\,x^{2\nu+1}=\frac{1}{i}\,F(ix)=(M_{-i}\circ F\circ M_{i})(x)\,\text{ for all }\,x\in[-1,1]\,.

In particular,

\displaystyle\psi^{\text{hyp}}(1)=\frac{1}{i}\,F(i).

(6.2.31)

Next, consider for example, the function
a:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1+H_{2}):\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Then
a\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{1})}
and
[-1,1]\ni\rho\mapsto h_{a,a}(\rho)=\frac{1}{2}(1+\rho^{2})
defines an
even
CCP
function, such that
h_{a,a}(0)=\frac{1}{2}>0
. With this example in mind, the
assumption in the second part of the following result is not empty.

Proposition 6.2.10
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
a,b,f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
. Then

a\otimes f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2k},\gamma_{2k})
and
b\otimes g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2k},\gamma_{2k})
, and

h_{a\otimes f,b\otimes g}=h_{a,b}\cdot h_{f,g}\,.

In particular, the product
h^{\text{ev}}\,h^{\text{odd}}
of a continuous, even
CCP function and a continuous, odd CCP function is an odd, strictly increasing
homeomorphic CCP function. If
(h^{\text{odd}})^{\prime}(0)>0
and

h^{\text{ev}}(0)>0
, then
(h^{\text{ev}}\,h^{\text{odd}})^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}
is real analytic. A product of two odd CCP functions is an even CCP
function.

If we apply
Theorem 6.2.2
to a pair of
k
-dimensional
Hermite polynomials and recall that
\{H_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}\}
actually is an
orthonormal basis in
L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
(cf. (
6.1.1
)), we
immediately reobtain another remarkable property of Hermite polynomials (cf.

[
113
, Lemma 1.1.1]
and
[
114
, Proposition 11.33]
).

Corollary 6.2.11
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho\in[-1,1]
,
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
and

\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
. If

{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho))
, then

h_{H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}}(\rho)=\mathbb{E}[H_{\alpha}(\textbf{X})\,H_{\beta}(\textbf{Y})]=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\rho^{|\alpha|}=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}}\,\rho^{\alpha_{i}}.

Remark 6.2.12

(
Noise stability
)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
f\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
and
A,B
Borel sets in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Let

\rho\in[-1,1]\setminus\{0\}
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho))
. Then
\text{sign}(\rho)\textbf{X}\sim N_{k}(0,I_{k})
. Consequently, (
) implies that

\displaystyle T_{\vartheta(|\rho|)}f_{\rho}(y)=\mathbb{E}[f(\rho\,y+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\,\textbf{X})]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}f(\rho\,y+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\,x)\gamma_{k}(\textup{d}x)=U_{\rho}f(y)

(6.2.32)

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where
U_{\rho}
is the Gaussian noise operator
(cf.
[
114
, Definition 11.12]
).
U_{\rho}

also is well-defined for
\rho=0
, with constant value

U_{0}f=\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{X})]
. Observe that (
6.2.32
) implies
that
U_{\rho}=T_{\vartheta(|\rho|)}M_{\rho}
, where the isometry

M_{\rho}=M_{\rho}^{-1}\in{\mathfrak{L}}(L^{2}(\gamma_{k}),L^{2}(\gamma_{k}))
is given
by
M_{\rho}f:=f_{\rho}
. A special case of (
) is given by

\mathbb{P}(\textbf{X}\in A,\textbf{Y}\in B)=\mathbb{E}[{1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{A}}(\textbf{X}){1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{B}}(\textbf{Y})]=h_{{1\hskip-2.84526pt1_{A}},1\hskip-2.84526pt1_{B}}(\rho)=\langle 1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{A},U_{\rho}1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{B}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}.

Therefore,
Theorem 6.2.2
(under inclusion of

Proposition
2.2.2
) encompasses the key concept of
Gaussian noise stability, most commonly introduced as
{\textbf{Stab}}_{\rho}[f]:=\langle f,U_{\rho}f\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}
(
\rho\in[-1,1]
,
f\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
).
Gaussian noise stability also comprises deep connections to geometry of minimal
surfaces, hypercontractivity, isoperimetric inequalities, communication complexity
and Gaussian copulas. A very comprehensive introductory processing of these
topics can be found in
[
114
, Chapter 11]
and the references therein,
including the seminal results of E. Mossel and J. Neeman.

In fact, it can be verified that
T_{t}
(and hence
U_{\rho}=T_{\vartheta(|\rho|)}M_{\rho}
) is even a
nuclear
operator,
implying that each
T_{t}
(and each
U_{\rho}
) in particular is a

compact
Hilbert-Schmidt operator! More precisely, we have

Proposition 6.2.13
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\geq 0
and
\rho\in(-1,1)\setminus\{0\}
.
Then both,
T_{t}\in\mathfrak{L}(L^{2}(\gamma_{k}),L^{2}(\gamma_{k}))
, and

U_{\rho}\in\mathfrak{L}(L^{2}(\gamma_{k}),L^{2}(\gamma_{k}))
are Hilbert-Schmidt
operators, satisfying

\|T_{t}\|_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}=\frac{1}{(1-e^{-2t})^{k/2}}\text{ and }\|U_{\rho}\|_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}=\frac{1}{(1-\rho^{2})^{k/2}}.

T_{t}
as well as
U_{\rho}
are even nuclear, and

(i)

\|T_{t}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}\leq\frac{1}{(1-e^{-t})^{k}}
.

(ii)

\|U_{\rho}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}\leq\frac{1}{(1-|\rho|)^{k}}
.

6.3
Examples of real CCP functions, Gaussian copulas and an extension of
Stein’s lemma

As was to be expected,
Theorem 6.2.2

and
Theorem 6.2.6
give us first non-trivial examples, such
as

h_{\text{sign},\text{sign}}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-1,1],x\mapsto\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}\,x\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2};x^{2}\big)

in the one-dimensional real case (implying the Grothendieck equality) and

h_{f_{2},f_{2}}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-1,1],\tau\mapsto\frac{\pi}{4}\tau\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},2;\tau^{2}\big)\,

in the one-dimensional complex case (implying the Haagerup equality), where

\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni x\mapsto f_{2}(x):=\begin{cases}\sqrt{2}\,\frac{x_{1}}{\|x\|_{2}}&\text{if }x\not=0\\
0&\text{if }x=0\end{cases}\,.

(cf.
[
62
, Lemma 3.2. and Proof of Theorem 3.1]
and
Example
7.3.4
).
If we namely apply
Theorem 4.3.1
to
m=1
and arbitrary
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we will
recognise that
Theorem 6.2.2
and Theorem

6.2.6
lead us to CCP functions

h_{f,f}
, where
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
is
bounded a.e.
. These
examples also include
[
25
, Lemma 2.1]
as a special case. More
precisely, we have:

Proposition 6.3.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\equiv(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})^{\top}\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho))
.
Consider the odd function

\mathbb{R}^{k}\ni x\mapsto f_{k}(x):=\begin{cases}\sqrt{k}\,\frac{x_{1}}{\|x\|_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}}&\text{if }x\not=0\\
0&\text{if }x=0\end{cases}\,.

Then
f_{k}\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}\cap L^{\infty}(\gamma_{k})
and

\|f_{k}\|_{\infty}=\sqrt{k}
. The function
h_{f_{k},f_{k}}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-1,1]
is an odd strictly increasing homeomorphism which is CCP
and satisfies
h_{f_{k},f_{k}}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
. Let

c_{k}
be defined as in
Lemma
4.2.5
. Then

0<h_{f_{k},f_{k}}^{\prime}(0)=c_{k}^{2}<1
.
h_{f_{k},f_{k}}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}
is
real analytic, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}h_{f_{k},f_{k}}(\rho)&=k\,\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{X_{1}\,Y_{1}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}\,\|\textbf{Y}\|_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\frac{\!\!\!\!\!\textbf{X}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}},\frac{\!\!\!\!\!\textbf{Y}}{\|\textbf{Y}\|_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}\big]\\
&=c_{k}^{2}\,\rho\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{k+2}{2};\rho^{2}\big)=c_{k}^{2}\,k\,!!\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{((2n-1)!!)^{2}}{(2n)!!\,(2n+k)!!}\,\rho^{2n+1}\end{split}

(6.3.33)

In particular, the function
[-1,1]\ni\rho\mapsto h_{f_{k},f_{k}}(\rho)-c_{k}^{2}\,\rho
is CCP as well.
If
|\rho|<1
, then

\displaystyle h_{f_{k},f_{k}}(\rho)=\sqrt{\frac{2k}{\pi}}\,c_{k}\,\rho\,\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(\sqrt{1-t^{2}})^{k-1}}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}\,t^{2}}}\,\textup{d}t\,.

(6.3.34)

Moreover,

\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{X_{i}\,Y_{i}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}\,\|\textbf{Y}\|_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}}\big]=\frac{c_{k}^{2}}{k}\,\rho\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{k+2}{2};\rho^{2}\big)\,\text{ and }\,\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{\!\!X_{i}^{2}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{k}}^{2}}\big]=\frac{1}{k}

for all
i\in[k]
.

Remark 6.3.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
c_{k}
be defined as in

Lemma
4.2.5
. Then
[
24
, Theorem 1]

in fact implies that

\frac{\pi}{2}\,c_{d}^{2}\leq K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\,\text{ for all }\,d\in\mathbb{N}_{3}\,.

Remark 6.3.3

(
Krivine rounding scheme reconsidered
)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Due to
Proposition
6.3.1
, the following set of
real-valued functions is non-empty:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{k}:=\big\{f:f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})},f\text{ is odd},h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)>0\big\}.

(6.3.35)

Let
f\in{\mathcal{G}}_{k}
. If in addition,
|f|=1
and

h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))

Lemma
6.2.9
implies that
\{f\circ\sqrt{2},f\circ\sqrt{2}\}

is a Krivine rounding scheme, introduced in
[
23
, Definition 2.1]

(since
h_{f,f}
coincides with the function
H_{f\circ\sqrt{2},f\circ\sqrt{2}}
,
introduced in
[
23
, Definition 2.1]
and
c(f\circ\sqrt{2},f\circ\sqrt{2})=h_{f,f}^{\text{hyp}}(1)\in(0,1)
). The latter fact should
be compared with
Theorem 6.4.14
!

Now it is no longer surprising that
Proposition
6.3.1

encompasses the Grothendieck equality and the Haagerup equality as particular cases.

Example 6.3.4

(
k=1
(Grothendieck)
)
.

Fix
\rho\in[-1,1]
. It is well-known that
\arcsin(\rho)=\rho\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2};\rho^{2}\big)
, whence

h_{f_{1},f_{1}}(\rho)=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin(\rho)
.

Example 6.3.5

(
k=2
(Haagerup)
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
u,v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that
\|u\|=1
and

\|v\|=1
. Let
Z\sim{\mathbb{C}}N_{n}(0,I_{n})
. The Haagerup equality
(
4.1.2
), together with (
4.1.3
)
implies that

\mathbb{E}[{\text{sign}}(u^{\ast}Z){\text{sign}}(\overline{v^{\ast}Z})]={\text{sign}}(u^{\ast}v)\,h_{f_{2},f_{2}}(|u^{\ast}v|).

In the following, we shed some light on the underlying structure of
the function
h_{\psi_{a},\psi_{b}}
, where
\psi_{p}:=1-2\,1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{J_{p}}\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
and
J_{p}:=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}(-\infty,p_{i}]
for all
p\equiv(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
(introduced in

Corollary
6.1.2
). Recall here that for any

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Sigma\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
and
\textbf{X}\equiv(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})^{\top}\sim N_{n}(0,\Sigma)
,

\Phi_{0,\Sigma}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\longrightarrow[0,1]
denotes the
n
-variate
distribution function of
X
, i.e.,

\Phi_{0,\Sigma}(x):=F_{\textbf{X}}(x)=\mathbb{P}\big(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\{X_{i}\leq x_{i}\}\big)\text{ for all }x\equiv(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,.

Proposition 6.3.6
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
\textbf{X}\equiv(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2k})^{\top}\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho))
. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
x\equiv(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2k})^{\top}={\text{vec}}(a,b)
. Then

\displaystyle h_{\psi_{a},\psi_{b}}(\rho)=4\Phi_{0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho)}(x)+1-2\Phi_{0,I_{k}}(a)-2\Phi_{0,I_{k}}(b).

(6.3.36)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\Phi_{0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho)}(x)=\Phi_{0,I_{k}}(a)\Phi_{0,I_{k}}(b)+\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}d_{\nu}(x;k)\,\rho^{\nu}\,,

where

\displaystyle d_{\nu}(x;k):=\sum_{m\in C(\nu,k)}\big(\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}=0}}}^{2k}\,\Phi(x_{i})\prod\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i=1}}{{m_{i}\not=0}}}^{2k}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m_{i}}}\varphi(x_{i})H_{m_{i}-1}(x_{i})\big).

Proposition
6.3.6

immediately gives us another significant example - which contains the Grothendieck
equality as a special case again. Due to the famous Theorem of Sklar, it
emerges from a lurking multivariate Gaussian copula; i.e., from a certain
finite-dimensional distribution function with uniformly distributed marginals
(cf., e.g.,
[
119
]
and the references therein).

Example 6.3.7

(
A lurking
\Sigma_{2k}(\rho)
-Gaussian copula
)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
m=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
,

\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})^{\top}\in(0,1)^{k}
and

\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})^{\top}\in(0,1)^{k}
. Let
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and

b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, where

a_{i}:=\Phi^{-1}(\alpha_{i})
and
b_{i}:=\Phi^{-1}(\beta_{i})
for all
i\in[k]
. Then

h_{\psi_{a},\psi_{b}}(\rho)=1-2\big(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}+\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{i}\big)+4\,c_{\Sigma_{2k}(\rho)}(\alpha,\beta)\text{ for all }\rho\in[-1,1],

where

(0,1)^{k}\ni(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})\mapsto c_{\Sigma_{2k}(\rho)}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}):=\Phi_{0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho)}(\Phi^{-1}(u_{1}),\ldots,\Phi^{-1}(u_{k}))

denotes the
2k
-dimensional Gaussian copula with respect to the correlation
matrix
\Sigma_{2k}(\rho)
(cf., e.g.,
[
119
]
). Consequently, if

(\alpha,\beta)=(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})
, it follows that
a=0
,

b=0
and

\displaystyle h_{\psi_{0},\psi_{0}}(\rho)=1-\tfrac{1}{2^{k-2}}+4\Phi_{0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho)}(0)=1-\tfrac{1}{2^{k-2}}+4\mathbb{P}\big(\bigcap_{i=1}^{2k}\{X_{i}\leq 0\}\big)

(6.3.37)

for all
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
\textbf{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{2k})^{\top}\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho))
(since
\Phi(0)=\tfrac{1}{2}
). In fact, if

(\alpha,\beta)=(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})
and
k=1
, then

\psi_{0}=\text{sign}-1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{\{0\}}
, implying that
(
6.3.37
) even reduces to the Grothendieck equality.
In order to recognise this, recall first that
(2l)!=2^{l}\,l!\,(2l-1)!!

for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, whence

\displaystyle((2l-1)!!)^{2}=\binom{2l}{l}\frac{(2l)!}{4^{l}}\,.

(6.3.38)

Consequently, the power series representation of the function

h_{\psi_{0},\psi_{0}}
, together with

Corollary
6.1.2
and
(
6.1.4
) implies that

\displaystyle\begin{split}4\mathbb{P}(X_{1}\leq 0,X_{2}\leq 0)-1&=h_{\text{sign},\text{sign}}(\rho)=h_{\psi_{0},\psi_{0}}(\rho)\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\langle\psi_{0},H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}})^{2}\,\rho^{n}=\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2l+1}H_{2l}^{2}(0)\,\rho^{2l+1}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Hermite_polynomials_at_zero}}}{{=}}\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}((2l-1)!!)^{2}\,\frac{\rho^{2l+1}}{(2l+1)!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:arcsin_part}}}{{=}}\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin(\rho)\end{split}

(6.3.39)

for all
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
(X_{1},X_{2})^{\top}\sim N_{2}(0,I_{2})

(cf. also
[
66
,
106
]
). Again, we recognise that the Hermite expansion
of the function
sign
- in
L^{2}(\gamma_{1})
- is given by

\displaystyle\text{sign}=\psi_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{l}\,\frac{(2l-1)!!}{\sqrt{(2l+1)!}}\,H_{2l+1}

(6.3.40)

(cf. (
6.1.11
)).

A further interesting one-dimensional example (
k=1
) is given by the
function
h_{\Phi,\Phi}
, where
\Phi=F_{X}
is the (continuous) distribution
function of a standard normally distributed random variable
X\sim N_{1}(0,1)
.
To this end, recall that the (continuous) random variable
U:=\Phi(X)\sim U(0,1)
is uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
, implying that
\Phi\in L^{2}(\gamma_{1})
, with
\langle\Phi,1\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}=\mathbb{E}[U]=\frac{1}{2}

and
\|\Phi\|_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[U^{2}]=Var(U)+\mathbb{E}^{2}[U]=\frac{1}{12}+\frac{1}{4}=\frac{1}{3}
(cf.
[
119
, Remark 2.17.]
). In particular,

\mathbb{E}[\kappa(X)]=0
, where
\kappa:=2\sqrt{3}\Phi-\sqrt{3}=\sqrt{3}(2\Phi-1)
. Now, we are ready to prove

Proposition 6.3.8
.

Let
X\sim N_{1}(0,1)
and
\kappa:=\sqrt{3}\big(2\Phi-1\big)
. Then the following
properties hold:

(i)

\frac{\textup{d}^{n}}{\textup{d}t^{n}}\mathbb{E}[\Phi(X+t)]=2^{-n/2}\,\varphi^{(n-1)}\big(\frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}\big)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }t\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(ii)

\mathbb{E}[\Phi(X+t)]=\Phi\big(\frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}\big)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }t\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(iii)

\mathbb{E}[\Phi(X)\,H_{2n+1}(X)]=(-1)^{n}\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi}}\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{4^{n}}\frac{1}{2n+1}{\binom{2n}{n}}}\,\sqrt{(\frac{1}{2})^{2n+1}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In particular,

\mathbb{E}[\kappa(X)\,H_{2n+1}(X)]=(-1)^{n}\,\sqrt{\frac{6}{\pi}}\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{4^{n}}\frac{1}{2n+1}{\binom{2n}{n}}}\,\sqrt{(\frac{1}{2})^{2n+1}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(iv)

h_{\Phi,\Phi}(\rho)=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\arcsin(\frac{\rho}{2})\text{ and }h_{\kappa,\kappa}(\rho)=\frac{6}{\pi}\,\arcsin(\frac{\rho}{2})\text{ for all }\rho\in[-1,1]
.

(v)

h_{\sqrt{3}\,\Phi,\sqrt{3}\,\Phi}=3\,h_{\Phi,\Phi}=\frac{3}{4}(1+\frac{2}{\pi}\,\arcsin(\frac{1}{2}\cdot))
is a strictly
increasing homeomorphic CCP function which maps
[-1,1]
onto

[\frac{1}{2},1]
and is neither odd nor even.
h_{\kappa,\kappa}
is an odd,
strictly increasing homeomorphic CCP function.
h_{\sqrt{3}\,\Phi,\sqrt{3}\,\Phi}^{-1}(t)=-2\cos(\frac{2\pi}{3}t)
for all
t\in[\frac{1}{2},1]
,

h_{\kappa,\kappa}^{-1}(s)=2\sin(\frac{\pi}{6}s)
and

\big(h_{\kappa,\kappa}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}(s)=2\sinh(\frac{\pi}{6}s)

for all
s\in[-1,1]
. In particular,
\big(h_{\kappa,\kappa}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)>1
.
h_{\kappa,\kappa}^{\text{hyp}}(\rho)=\frac{6}{\pi}\sinh^{-1}(\frac{\rho}{2})
for all
\rho\in[-1,1]
.

(vi)

\kappa
is odd,
\|\kappa\|_{\gamma_{1}}=1
and

h_{\kappa,\kappa}^{\prime}(0)=\frac{3}{\pi}>0
. Moreover,
\kappa\in L^{\infty}(\gamma_{1})
, and
\|\kappa\|_{\infty}=\sqrt{3}
.

We will soon realise that
Theorem 6.2.2
actually
reflects a characterisation of the class of all real continuous CCP functions (see Theorem

6.4.2
).

Within the scope of our analysis of the Grothendieck constants, we need

invertible
CCP functions, implying that we may ignore even CCP functions,
defined on
[-1,1]
(since these are non-injective). However, thanks to

Theorem 5.2.3
,
[-1,1]\ni\rho\mapsto\rho\,h(\rho)
is an odd CCP
function for any continuous, even CCP function
h
. In fact, we will recognise next that in particular
any CCP function
h_{f,f}
, which satisfies
h_{f,f}(-1)<1
induces canonically
an odd CCP function
h_{f^{\times},f^{\times}}
, where
f^{\times}\in S_{H}
is odd,
H:=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
. In the complex case it seems that we even have to use odd
functions, to avoid calculations with non-positive semidefinite entrywise absolute values of
correlation matrices (cf. (
7.1.14
) and
Theorem 7.3.3
-
(ii)
).

Proposition 6.3.9

(
Odd CCP transform
)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
. Consider the odd function

\mathbb{R}^{k}\ni x\mapsto f^{\text{odd}}(x):=\frac{f(x)-f(-x)}{2}
(the odd part of
f
).
Then
f
is odd if and only if
f^{\text{odd}}=f
. In particular,
(f^{\text{odd}})^{\text{odd}}=f^{\text{odd}}
.
f
is even if and only if
f^{\text{odd}}=0
. Moreover,

f^{\text{odd}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
and

(i)

\displaystyle\|f^{\text{odd}}\|_{\gamma_{k}}^{2}=\frac{h_{f,f}(1)-h_{f,f}(-1)}{2}\geq 0\,.

(ii)

f
is even
\lambda_{k}
-a.s. if and only if
h_{f,f}(-1)=h_{f,f}(1)
.

(iii)

f
is odd
\lambda_{k}
-a.s. if and only if
h_{f,f}(-1)=-h_{f,f}(1)
.

(iv)

Assume that
h_{f,f}(1)>h_{f,f}(-1)
. Put

\displaystyle f^{\times}:=\sqrt{\frac{2}{h_{f,f}(1)-h_{f,f}(-1)}}\,f^{\text{odd}}\,.

(6.3.41)

Then
f
is not even,
\|f^{\times}\|_{\gamma_{k}}=1
, and
h_{f^{\times},f^{\times}}=\frac{2}{h_{f,f}(1)-h_{f,f}(-1)}\,h_{f^{\text{odd}},f^{\text{odd}}}
is an odd CCP function.

Example 6.3.10
.

Since
\arcsin(\frac{1}{2})=\frac{\pi}{6}
,

Proposition
6.3.8
-
(iv)
implies that

h_{\Phi,\Phi}(1)=\frac{1}{3}
and
h_{\Phi,\Phi}(-1)=\frac{1}{6}
, whence

\Phi^{\times}=\sqrt{3}(2\Phi-1)=\kappa\,.

Example 6.3.11
.

Let
[-1,1]\ni\rho\mapsto C^{\text{Ga}}(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2};\rho)

denote the bivariate Gaussian copula with Pearson’s correlation coefficient
\rho
as
parameter, evaluated at
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
. Then

C^{\text{Ga}}(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2};\rho)=\Phi_{\Sigma_{2}(\rho)}(\Phi^{-1}(\frac{1}{2}),\Phi^{-1}(\frac{1}{2}))=\mathbb{P}(X\leq 0,Y\leq 0)=h_{g,g}(\rho)\,,

where
\Phi_{\Sigma_{2}(\rho)}
denotes the bivariate distribution function of the
random vector
(X,Y)^{\top}\sim N_{2}(0,\Sigma_{2}(\rho))
and

g:=1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{(-\infty,0]}
. Thus,
g^{\text{odd}}=-\frac{1}{2}\,\text{sign}
,

h_{g,g}(1)=\mathbb{P}(X\leq 0)=\Phi(0)=\frac{1}{2}
and
h_{g,g}(-1)=\mathbb{P}(X\leq 0,-X\leq 0)=\mathbb{P}(X=0)=0
.
Consequently,

g^{\times}=-\text{sign}=2\,1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{(-\infty,0)}-1+1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{\{0\}}\,,

and it follows that
h_{g^{\times},g^{\times}}=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin
(on
[-1,1]
).

Regarding an explicit calculation of the correlation coefficients

\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{X})g(\textbf{Y})]
, induced by a pair of non-linearly transformed
Gaussian random vectors and given “sufficiently smooth” functions
f
and
g
,
we implement a few facts from the theory of distributions and test function
spaces. A detailed in-depth introduction to these “generalised functions” and
test function spaces and their analysis is provided by e. g.

[
65
,
81
,
136
]
. To this end, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
f\in L^{1}_{{\text{loc}}}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
(i.e.,
f
is locally integrable) and
\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
be an arbitrary test function, where
{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
denotes
the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing test functions on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Put

\langle\psi,\Lambda_{f}\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}f(x)\psi(x)\textup{d}^{k}{x}\,.

Observe that the latter symbol
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the
duality bracket on
{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
and not
an inner product of Hilbert space elements. When there is no ambiguity, we
adopt the common habit to identify the tempered distribution
\Lambda_{f}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
with
f\in L^{1}_{{\text{loc}}}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
itself. More
generally, recall that tempered distributions and their derivatives are
elements of the dual space
{\mathcal{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
, such
as the Dirac delta distribution
\delta_{0}=\delta
, defined via

\langle\psi,\delta\rangle:=\psi(0)
for any
\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
.
Observe that there is no
g\in L^{1}_{{\text{loc}}}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that
\delta=\Lambda_{g}
.

Let us quickly recall how differentiation of tempered distributions is
defined. It originates from a reiteration of the integration by parts formula
and is also known as “weak differentiation” (cf. e. g.
[
136
, Chapter 6.12]
):

\displaystyle\langle\psi,D^{n}u\rangle:=(-1)^{|n|}\langle D^{n}\psi,u\rangle=(-1)^{|n|}\langle\psi,D^{n-1}(Du)\rangle

(6.3.42)

for all
\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k},u\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
. Consequently, since
H_{n}\,\varphi_{k}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
for all

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
and
D^{n}u\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
for all
u\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
, (
6.1.3
) in particular implies:

\langle H_{n}\,\varphi_{k},u\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\langle\varphi_{k},D^{n}u\rangle\text{ for all }u\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}^{\prime}\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}\,.

Given an arbitrary compact subset

K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
f\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
, it follows that

\int_{K}|f|\,\textup{d}\lambda_{k}=\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{K}(x)\exp(\frac{1}{2}\|x\|^{2})\,|f(x)|\,\gamma_{k}(\textup{d}x)\leq c_{K}\,\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}\,,

where
c_{K}:=\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{K}e^{\|x\|^{2}}\,\gamma_{k}(\textup{d}x)=\sqrt{2\pi}\int_{K}\exp(\frac{1}{2}\|x\|^{2})\lambda_{k}(\textup{d}x)<\infty
. Consequently,
L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\subseteq L^{1}_{{\text{loc}}}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
implying that for any
\textbf{X}\sim N_{k}(0,I_{k})

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\textbf{X})f(\textbf{X})]=\langle H_{n},f\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=\langle H_{n}\,\varphi_{k},\Lambda_{f}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\langle\varphi_{k},D^{n}\Lambda_{f}\rangle\text{ for all }f\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}\,.

(6.3.43)

Example 6.3.12

(
n
’th weak derivative of
\Lambda_{1\hskip-2.84526pt1_{[0,\infty)}}
)
.

Let
k=1
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. A very easy proof by induction on
n
, based on
(
6.3.42
), together with (
6.1.2
)
firstly reveals that

\langle\varphi,D^{n}\delta\rangle=(-1)^{n}\,\varphi^{(n)}(0)=\frac{\sqrt{n!}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,H_{n}(0)\,.

(
6.1.4
) therefore implies that

\langle\varphi,D^{2l}\delta\rangle=(-1)^{l}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,(2l-1)!!\,\text{ for all }l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,.

Similarly, (
6.3.42
) implies that
D\Lambda_{1\hskip-2.84526pt1_{[0,\infty)}}=\delta
. Since
\text{sign}=2\,1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{[0,\infty)}-1
(
\gamma_{1}
-almost surely) and

\langle H_{2l+1},1\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}=\langle H_{2l+1},H_{0}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}=\delta_{2l+1,0}=0
, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle H_{2l+1},\text{sign}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}

\displaystyle=2\langle H_{2l+1},1\hskip-7.11317pt1_{[0,\infty)}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Hermite_f_bracket}}}{{=}}\frac{2}{\sqrt{(2l+1)!}}\langle\varphi,D^{2l+1}\Lambda_{1\hskip-2.84526pt1_{[0,\infty)}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{(2l+1)!}}\langle\varphi,D^{2l}\delta\rangle=(-1)^{l}\,\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\frac{(2l-1)!!}{\sqrt{(2l+1)!}}

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. This outcome – which is solely based on a multiple
weak differentiation of
\Lambda_{1\hskip-2.84526pt1_{[0,\infty)}}
– should be compared now
with (the derivation of) (
6.3.39
) !

(
6.3.43
) can be strongly simplified if
f
is
smooth (cf. also
Proposition
6.1.3
). More
precisely, if
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0},D^{n}f\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{k})
for all

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
, satisfying
0\leq|n|\leq N
, and if
\textbf{X}\sim N_{k}(0,I_{k})
, then
D^{n}\Lambda_{f}=\Lambda_{D^{n}f}
(cf.

[
136
, Chapter 6.13]
), and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[H_{n}(\textbf{X})f(\textbf{X})]=\langle H_{n},f\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\mathbb{E}[D^{n}f(\textbf{X})]\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}\,,\text{ with }|n|\leq N\,.

(6.3.44)

In fact, even more can be said. Recall that (by construction of Sobolev spaces) the latter
equality also holds without the smoothness assumption, if we just assume that
f\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\cap W_{{\text{loc}}}^{N,1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, so that in this case
D^{n}f
denotes the
distributional
n
’th derivative in
L_{{\text{loc}}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
instead (cf. e. g.

[
65
, Chapter 3.1]
). Independent of an application of Theorem

6.2.2
to the primary topic of our work, it implies further
non-trivial consequences including a generalisation of Stein’s Lemma (cf.

[
97
, Theorem 1 and Example 1]
) and a certain “decorrelation property”
of harmonic functions. We only have to combine (
6.3.44
) and

Theorem 6.2.2
, resulting at once in

Proposition 6.3.13
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho))
. Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
. Then

{\text{cov}}(f(\textbf{X}),g(\textbf{Y}))=\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}\big(\sum_{n\in C(\nu,k)}\frac{1}{n!}\,\langle\varphi_{k},D^{n}\Lambda_{f}\rangle\,\langle\varphi_{k},D^{n}\Lambda_{g}\rangle\big)\,\rho^{\nu}\,.

If in addition
(f,g)\in W_{{\text{loc}}}^{N,1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\times W_{{\text{loc}}}^{N,1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, or if
(D^{n}f,D^{n}g)\in(L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{k}))\times(L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{k}))
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
, satisfying

0\leq|n|\leq N
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, then

{\text{cov}}(f(\textbf{X}),g(\textbf{Y}))=\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\big(\sum_{n\in C(\nu,k)}\frac{1}{n!}\,\mathbb{E}[D^{n}f(\textbf{X})]\mathbb{E}[D^{n}g(\textbf{Y})]\big)\rho^{\nu}+\sum_{\nu=N+1}^{\infty}\big(\sum_{n\in C(\nu,k)}\frac{1}{n!}\,\langle\varphi_{k},D^{n}\Lambda_{f}\rangle\,\langle\varphi_{k},D^{n}\Lambda_{g}\rangle\big)\,\rho^{\nu}\,.

In particular, if
N=1
, then

{\text{cov}}(f(\textbf{X}),Y_{i})=\rho\,\mathbb{E}[\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}(\textbf{X})]

for all
i\in[k]
, respectively

\mathbb{E}[f(\textbf{X})\textbf{Y}]=\rho\,\mathbb{E}[\nabla f(\textbf{X})].

If
N=2
, then

{\text{cov}}(f(\textbf{X}),\|\textbf{Y}\|^{2})=\rho^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[\triangle f(\textbf{X})].

In the one-dimensional case (i. e.,
k=1
), a direct application of

Proposition
6.3.13
, respectively
(
6.3.44
), implies two further results which are of their own
interest. In particular, our approach enables the provision of a quick and short
proof of the following generalisation of Stein’s Lemma to standard Gaussian
random powers (cf.
[
103
, Theorem 1]
):

Corollary 6.3.14
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f\in L^{2}(\gamma_{1})
and
X\sim N_{1}(0,1)
be given. If

D^{l}f\equiv f^{(l)}\in L^{2}(\gamma_{1})\cap C(\mathbb{R})
for all

0\leq l\leq m
, then

\mathbb{E}[f(X)X^{m}]=\mathbb{E}[(-i)^{m}\,H_{m}(iD)(f(X))]=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\left\lfloor m/2\right\rfloor}\binom{m}{2\nu}(2\nu-1)!!\,\mathbb{E}[D^{m-2\nu}f(X)]\,.

Corollary 6.3.15
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
f,g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{1})
and
\rho\in(-1,1)
.
Then

h_{f,g}^{(\nu)}(\rho)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\,\langle\varphi,D^{n}(D^{\nu}\Lambda_{f})\rangle\langle\varphi,D^{n}(D^{\nu}\Lambda_{g})\rangle\rho^{n}\,.

In particular, if in addition
(f,g)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, then

h_{f,g}^{(\nu)}(\rho)=h_{f^{(\nu)},g^{(\nu)}}(\rho)\text{ for all }\rho\in(-1,1).

Remark 6.3.16
.

Firstly, observe that the strong impact of the standard (multivariate)
Gaussian

law, is reflected in (
6.3.43
), primarily implied by the fact that the
Gaussian density function
\varphi_{k}
and hence each
H_{n}\,\varphi_{k}
, is rapidly decreasing:

H_{n}\,\varphi_{k}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
(since each
H_{n}
is a
polynomial). This fact and the structure of the (multivariate) Hermite polynomials (which
itself is also induced by the structure of
\varphi_{k}
) namely enables a reiterated use of
the integration by parts formula in the smooth case, respectively a use of the Sobolev space

W_{{\text{loc}}}^{N,1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
in the non-smooth case, implying the transition of each
inner product
\langle H_{n},f\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}
on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
into
the duality bracket
\langle\varphi_{k},D^{n}\,\Lambda_{f}\rangle
on

{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{k}^{\prime}
. The latter, however, seems to be more
convenient for performing specific computations.

6.4
Upper bounds of
K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
and inversion of real CCP functions

Our next step is to embed Grothendieck’s original approach as well as
Krivine’s improvement into a general framework. We are going to show that we
may
substitute
the Grothendieck function
h_{\text{sign},\text{sign}}=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin
through
invertible
CCP functions
h_{f,f}
,
generated by
bounded
functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
(see

Theorem 6.4.9
and
Theorem 6.4.14
).
Various proofs of Krivine’s main result (
K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{\pi}{2\ln(1+\sqrt{2})}\approx 1.782
) are set out in detail in

[
75
, Section 5]
,
[
82
, proof of Lemma 10.5]
and
[
161
]
. We
will recognise soon that inverses of odd CCP functions also lead to a further
crucial construction of correlation matrices, lurking in the following important
implication of
Lemma
5.1.12
:

Theorem 6.4.1
.

Let
r>0
,
0<c\leq r
and
0\not=\psi\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
. Then

\psi_{\text{abs}}\big|_{[0,r]}
is strictly increasing and

\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)>0
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
there exist

\alpha_{k}\equiv\alpha^{\psi,c}_{k}\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
and
\beta_{k}\equiv\beta^{\psi,c}_{k}\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
, such that the following properties
hold:

(i)

If
\textbf{X}\sim N_{k}(0,I_{k})
, then

\sqrt{\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)}\,\mathbb{E}[\alpha_{k}(\textbf{X})]=\text{sign}(\psi(0))\sqrt{|\psi(0)|}
and

\sqrt{\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)}\,\mathbb{E}[\beta_{k}(\textbf{X})]=\sqrt{|\psi(0)|}
. In particular,
\mathbb{E}[\alpha_{k}(\textbf{X})]=\text{sign}(\psi(0))\mathbb{E}[\beta_{k}(\textbf{X})]
.

(ii)

If
c<r
, then
[-c,c]\subseteq(-r,r)
and

\displaystyle\psi(c\rho)=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)h_{\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}}(\rho)\text{ for all }\rho\in[-1,1].

(6.4.45)

In particular,
\psi(c)=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\langle\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}
.

(iii)

If
c\leq r
, then

\psi_{\text{abs}}(c\rho)=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\,h_{\alpha_{k},\alpha_{k}}(\rho)=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\,h_{\beta_{k},\beta_{k}}(\rho)\text{ for all }\rho\in[-1,1].

(iv)

If
c<r
,
H
is an arbitrary
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert space, then there
exists a
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
such that for any
u,v\in S_{H}

\displaystyle\psi(c\langle u,v\rangle_{H})=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\langle\psi_{u}(\alpha_{k}),\psi_{v}(\beta_{k})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\langle a_{u},b_{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\,,

(6.4.46)

where for any
w\in S_{H}
,
\|a_{w}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\|b_{w}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)
and
\psi_{w}:L^{2}(\gamma_{k})\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}
is
a mapping which satisfies
\psi_{w}(S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})})\subseteq S_{\mathbb{H}}
.
In particular,
\psi(c)=\langle a_{w},b_{w}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}
for all
w\in S_{H}
.

If
c\leq r
, then

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{abs}}(c\langle u,v\rangle_{H})

\displaystyle=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\langle\psi_{u}(\alpha_{k}),\psi_{v}(\alpha_{k})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\langle\psi_{u}(\beta_{k}),\psi_{v}(\beta_{k})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle=\langle a_{u},a_{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\langle b_{u},b_{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\,,

If we combine
Theorem 5.2.3
, Theorem

6.2.2
,
Proposition
6.3.9
and Theorem

6.4.1
, (iii), a
further significant characterisation of continuous odd CCP functions follows at
once:

Theorem 6.4.2

(
CCP representation theorem
)
.

Let
\psi:[-1,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous odd function and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\psi
is a CCP function.

(ii)

\psi=h_{f,f}
for some odd
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
.

Theorem 6.4.1
-
(iv)
,
together with
Lemma
6.2.9
directly enriches us
with another crucial result, related to a construction of real quantum
correlation matrices which actually include lurking upper bounds of
K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
.
At this point we should especially recall the representation
(
6.2.31
) if the sign condition is
present.

Corollary 6.4.3
.

Let
m,n,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
be odd. Assume that

h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
. Put

c(f):=h_{f,f}^{\text{hyp}}(1)\,.

Then
c(f)\in(0,1)
and

h_{f,f}^{-1}[c(f)S]\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}\,\text{ for all }\,S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}\,.

If

\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}M&S\\
S^{\top}&N\end{pmatrix}\in C(m+n;\mathbb{R})
is an arbitrary real
(m+n)\times(m+n)

correlation matrix (with block elements
M\in C(m;\mathbb{R}),N\in C(n;\mathbb{R})
and

S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}
), then

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}(h_{f,f}^{-1})_{\text{abs}}[c(f)M]&h_{f,f}^{-1}[c(f)S]\\[6.99997pt]
h_{f,f}^{-1}[c(f)S^{\top}]&(h_{f,f}^{-1})_{\text{abs}}[c(f)N]\end{pmatrix}\in C(m+n;\mathbb{R})

(6.4.47)

again is an
(m+n)\times(m+n)
correlation matrix with real entries.

If
\psi:D_{\mathbb{F}}\longrightarrow D_{\mathbb{F}}
is an arbitrary CCP function
and
\Sigma\in C(m+n;\mathbb{F})
is an arbitrary
m+n
-correlation matrix, then

\psi[\Sigma]=\begin{pmatrix}\psi[\Gamma_{H}(u,u)]&\psi[\Gamma_{H}(u,v)]\\[1.99997pt]
\psi[\Gamma_{H}(u,v)^{\ast}]&\psi[\Gamma_{H}(v,v)]\end{pmatrix}\in C(m+n;\mathbb{F})

again is a correlation matrix. Consequently, it follows that
\psi[\Gamma_{H}(u,u)]=\Gamma_{K}(x,x)\in C(m;\mathbb{F})
,
\psi[\Gamma_{H}(v,v)]=\Gamma_{K}(y,y)\in C(n;\mathbb{F})
and
\psi[\Gamma_{H}(u,v)]=\Gamma_{K}(x,y)\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}(\mathbb{F})
for some Hilbert space
K
over
\mathbb{F}

and some
(x,y)\in S_{K}^{m}\times S_{K}^{n}
(due to

Corollary
3.1.5
). However, a
considerably stronger result holds (cf. also
(
6.4.47
)):

Theorem 6.4.4
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
A\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(\mathbb{R})
,
S\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R})
and
B\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.
Consider the block matrix

M:=\begin{pmatrix}A&S\\
S^{\top}&B\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{M}_{m+n}(\mathbb{R})\,.

Let
0<r<\infty
and
f,g:(-r,r)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

be two functions, such that
(-r,r)\ni x\mapsto f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}a_{\nu}x^{\nu}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
and
(-r,r)\ni x\mapsto g(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}b_{\nu}x^{\nu}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
.
Let
q\in C^{\omega}((-r,r))
, such that

\displaystyle|c_{\nu}|\leq\sqrt{|a_{\nu}|\,|b_{\nu}|}\text{ for all }\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,,

(6.4.48)

where
c_{\nu}:=\frac{q^{(\nu)}(0)}{\nu!}
.
Then

q\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
, and the following properties
hold:

(i)

If
M\in\mathbb{M}_{m+n}([-r,r])^{+}
is positive semidefinite, and if any
entry of the matrices
A,S
and
B
is an element of
[-r,r]
, then also

\begin{pmatrix}f_{\text{abs}}[A]&\widetilde{q}[S]\\
\widetilde{q}[S]^{\top}&g_{\text{abs}}[B]\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{M}_{m+n}([-r,r])^{+}

is positive semidefinite, where
\widetilde{q}
is defined as in

Lemma
5.1.12
. In particular, if
0<c^{\ast}\leq r

is a root of
f_{\text{abs}}-1
, then

\widetilde{q}[c^{\ast}\Gamma]\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}\text{ for all }\Gamma\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}\,.

(ii)

If
M\in C(m+n;\mathbb{R})
is a real correlation matrix, then also

\begin{pmatrix}h_{\alpha,\alpha}[A]&h_{\alpha,\beta}[S]\\
h_{\alpha,\beta}[S^{\top}]&h_{\beta,\beta}[B]\end{pmatrix}\in C(m+n;\mathbb{R})

is a real correlation matrix for all
\alpha,\beta\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
.
In particular,

\displaystyle h_{\alpha,\beta}[\cdot]:{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}\text{ for all }\alpha,\beta\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}.

(6.4.49)

(iii)

For all
\alpha,\beta\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
, for all Hilbert spaces
H
and

u,v\in S_{H}
there exist
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, such that

h_{\alpha,\beta}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})=\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{d}}=x^{\top}y\,.

However, we will recognise that Theorem

6.4.4
cannot be fully transferred to the
complex field, so that we have to distinguish carefully between the real case
and the complex case here (cf.
Proposition
7.1.7
). If we link
(
5.1.4
) and Theorem

6.4.4
,
we obtain

Corollary 6.4.5
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N},A\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R})
and

\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}M&S\\
S^{\top}&N\end{pmatrix}\in C(m+n;\mathbb{R})

be an arbitrary real
(m+n)\times(m+n)
correlation matrix (with block elements

M\in C(m;\mathbb{R}),N\in C(n;\mathbb{R})
and
S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}
). Let

r>0
and
0\not=\psi\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
. If

0<c\leq r
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{\text{abs}}[c\,M]&\widetilde{\psi}[c\,S]\\[5.0pt]
\widetilde{\psi}[c\,S]^{\top}&\psi_{\text{abs}}[c\,N]\end{pmatrix}\in C(m+n;\mathbb{R})

(6.4.50)

again is a correlation matrix with real entries. In particular,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)}\widetilde{\psi}[cS]\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}\,\text{ for all }\,S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}\,.

(6.4.51)

If we apply Theorem

6.4.1
, (iv) and
(
6.4.51
) to the

inverses
of invertible functions in
W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
, Bolzano’s
intermediate value theorem from calculus immediately implies a further crucial
result. To this end, recall also (
5.1.3
) and
(
5.1.4
).

Theorem 6.4.6

(
Real inner product rounding
)
.

Let
\psi:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-1,1]
be a bijective real function. Assume
that

\psi{|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))\text{ and }\psi^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1)).

Then
|\psi^{-1}(0)|=(\psi^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)})_{\text{abs}}(0)\leq(\psi^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)})_{\text{abs}}(1)
.
Assume that

|\psi^{-1}(0)|<1<(\psi^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)})_{\text{abs}}(1).

Then there is a unique number
c^{\ast}\in(0,1)
, such that

(\psi^{-1})_{\text{abs}}(c^{\ast})=1
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. There is
(\alpha,\beta)\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}\times S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
(dependent on
c^{\ast}
and
k
)
such that for any separable
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert space
H
and any
u,v\in S_{H}
,
the following statements apply:

(i)

\displaystyle\langle u,v\rangle_{H}=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\psi(\rho_{u,v})

(6.4.52)

where
\rho_{u,v}:=h_{\alpha,\beta}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})
.

(ii)

c^{\ast}=\psi(\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}).

(iii)

Suppose that
\psi=h_{f,g}
for some
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
and
f,g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{\nu})
.
If
\rho_{u,v}\in(-1,1)
, then

c^{\ast}\,\langle u,v\rangle_{H}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\nu}(1-\rho_{u,v}^{2})^{\nu/2}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}f(x)g(y)\exp\big(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}-2\rho_{u,v}\langle x,y\rangle_{2}}{2(1-\rho_{u,v}^{2})}\big)\textup{d}^{\nu}x\,\textup{d}^{\nu}y\,.

If
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(x,y)\in S_{H}^{m}\times S_{H}^{n}
, then there exist
m+n

\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued random vectors
\textbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{X}_{m},\textbf{Y}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{Y}_{n}
, such that
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X}_{i},\textbf{Y}_{j})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\rho_{ij}))
for all
(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]
,
and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{H}(x,y)=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}h_{f,g}[R]=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\,\mathbb{E}[{\textbf{P}}_{f}{\textbf{Q}}_{g}^{\top}]=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\,\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}({\textbf{P}}_{f},{\textbf{Q}}_{g})],

(6.4.53)

where
({\textbf{P}}_{f})_{i}:=f(\textbf{X}_{i})
,

({\textbf{Q}}_{g})_{j}:=g(\textbf{Y}_{j})
,
\rho_{ij}:=h_{\alpha,\beta}(\langle x_{i},y_{j}\rangle_{H})
and
R:=h_{\alpha,\beta}[\Gamma_{H}(x,y)]=(\rho_{ij})_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]}\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
.

(iv)

Moreover,
c^{\ast}\langle u,v\rangle_{H}\in(-1,1)
for all
u,v\in S_{H}
and

\displaystyle\text{tr}(A^{\top}S)=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\,\text{tr}(A^{\top}\,\psi[Q_{c^{\ast},\psi}])

(6.4.54)

for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
A\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R})
, for all
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
, where
Q_{c^{\ast},\psi}:=\psi^{-1}[c^{\ast}\,S]\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
.

It is far from being trivial that it is possible to transfer Theorem

6.4.6

from the real field
\mathbb{R}
to the complex field
\mathbb{C}
; at least if
f=g
is odd
(cf.
Theorem 7.3.2
).
In order to achieve this, we have to develop and implement certain non-trivial
structural properties of the class of complex Hermite polynomials
(cf.
Theorem 7.2.3
and Theorem

7.3.1
below).

If
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
A\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{F})
, then
A^{\ast\,\nu}

denotes the
\nu
-th entrywise power of the matrix
A
(in terms of Schur
multiplication), where
A^{\ast\,0}:={\bf{1}}_{m}{\bf{1}}_{n}^{\ast}
is the (rank 1)

m\times n
matrix of all ones, where
{\bf{1}}_{l}:=(1,1,\ldots,1)^{\top}\in\mathbb{F}^{l},l\in\mathbb{N}
(by adapting the convention that
0^{0}:=1
- cf.

[
86
, Remark 9.2]
).

Now, we are fully prepared to embed both, Grothendieck’s original estimation

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\sinh(\frac{\pi}{2})\approx 2.301
, and Krivine’s original estimation

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{\pi}{2\ln(1+\sqrt{2})}\approx 1.782
into a general framework.
In particular, we will provide a further proof of the Grothendieck inequality itself
(see
Theorem 6.4.9
below).
Moreover, we will make a cute use of the little Grothendieck inequality, yielding
a quite surprising outcome. To this end, we have to work with functions
h_{f,g}
,
which are “generated” by
bounded functions
f,g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\lambda_{k})\equiv L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
(
6.2.20
) obviously implies that

|h_{f,g}(\rho)|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\,\|g\|_{\infty}\,\text{ for all }\,f,g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\,\text{ and }\,\rho\in[-1,1]\,.

Remark 6.4.7
.

In statistical machine learning a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow\{-1,1\},k\in\mathbb{N}
is a particular example of a mapping from some general domain of
definition to the set
\{-1,1\}
, where the latter is known as “concept”
(cf. e.g.
[
148
]
). The concept
f
obviously satisfies the condition

\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}=1=\|f\|_{\infty}
.

We also need the following important result which holds for both fields
interchangeably.

Lemma 6.4.8
.

Let
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
and
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
R
be a

m
-dimensional random vector in
(\mathbb{F}\cap\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{m}
and
S
be a

n
-dimensional random vector in
(\mathbb{F}\cap\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{n}
, such that

\mathbb{E}[{\textbf{R}}{\textbf{S}}^{\ast}]
exists. Then

\|B\ast\mathbb{E}[{\textbf{R}}{\textbf{S}}^{\ast}]^{\ast\nu}\|^{\mathbb{F}}_{\infty,1}\leq\|B\|^{\mathbb{F}}_{\infty,1}\text{ for all }B\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{F})\text{ and }\nu\in\mathbb{N}\,.

Theorem 6.4.9
.

Let
k,m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
f,g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Put
r_{\infty}\equiv r_{\infty}(f,g):=\|f\|_{\infty}\,\|g\|_{\infty}
.
Then:

(i)

\displaystyle|{\text{tr}}(A^{\top}h_{f,g}[S])|\leq r_{\infty}\,\|A\|_{\infty,1}\text{ for all }A\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R})\text{ and }S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}

(6.4.55)

and

\displaystyle|{\text{tr}}(A^{\top}h_{f,f}[\Sigma])|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\max\limits_{x\in[-1,1]^{n}}|x^{\top}Ax|\text{ for all }A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\text{ and }\Sigma\in C(n;\mathbb{R}).

(6.4.56)

(ii)

Assume that
r_{\infty}>0
and
f
or
g
is odd. Let

h_{f,g}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be continuous and injective. Then

h_{f,g}
is a homeomorphism which is either strictly increasing or strictly
decreasing and satisfies
h_{f,g}([-1,1])=[-r,r]
, where
r\equiv r_{k}(f,g):=\max\{-h_{f,g}(1),h_{f,g}(1)\}=\max\{-\langle f,g\rangle_{\gamma_{k}},\langle f,g\rangle_{\gamma_{k}}\}
. Moreover,
0<r\leq r_{\infty}
. Assume that

h_{f,g}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
. Then the following
two statements hold:

(ii-1)

If
\big(h_{f,g}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r)>1
, then there is exactly one number
0<c^{\ast}_{k}\equiv c^{\ast}_{k}(f,g)<r
, such that
\big(h_{f,g}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(c^{\ast}_{k})=1
and

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{r_{\infty}}{c^{\ast}_{k}}\,.

(6.4.57)

(ii-2)

If
r=r_{\infty}
, then there exists a unique number
0<\gamma^{\ast}_{k}\equiv\gamma^{\ast}_{k}(f,g)\in(0,r]
, such that

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}=\big(h_{f,g}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(\gamma^{\ast}_{k})\leq\min\Big\{\frac{r}{c^{\ast}_{k}}\,,\big(h_{f,g}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r)\Big\}\,.

(6.4.58)

Inequality (
6.4.56
), together with
the equality (
3.2.13
) allows us to recover as special case
(applied to
f=\text{sign}
) an interesting result of Y. Nesterov, yet
without having to make use of their random hyperplane rounding technique
(cf.
[
110
]
):

Corollary 6.4.10
.

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
be positive semidefinite and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
be odd. Then

\displaystyle h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)\sup\limits_{\Sigma\in C(n;\mathbb{R})}{\text{tr}}(A\Sigma)

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{\Sigma\in C(n;\mathbb{R})}{\text{tr}}(A\,h_{f,f}[\Sigma])\leq\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\sup\limits_{x\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}x^{\top}Ax\leq\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\,\|A\|_{\infty,1}\,.

In particular,

\displaystyle 0\leq h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\,\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\,.

(6.4.59)

In a similar vain, we directly obtain a further (and very short) proof
for the value of the real little Grothendieck constant
k_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
. To this end, we
only have to combine (
6.4.55
) and

Proposition
6.3.1
:

Corollary 6.4.11

(
Grothendieck, 1953
)
.

k_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}=\frac{\pi}{2}.

Remark 6.4.12
.

A direct estimation of (
6.2.25
) leads to the upper bound

k\,\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\,(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}})^{2}=\frac{2k}{\pi}\,\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}
, which, however, strongly depends
on the dimension
k\in\mathbb{N}
. That upper bound, viewed as a function of
k
is even strictly
increasing. Our application of the little Grothendieck inequality implies the non-trivial
result that
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)
actually is bounded above by

\frac{2}{\pi}\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}
“uniformly”. Moreover, if
f
were an even function,
then (
6.4.59
) would be trivial, since

h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)=0
.

Theorem 6.4.9
-
(i)
also implies a remarkable
property of CCP functions. To this end, let
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
and

h=h_{f,f}
be an arbitrary CCP function (not necessarily odd !), generated
by some
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Firstly, note that in any case,

\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{1}{K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:known_upper_bounds_of_the_complex_GT_constant}}}{{<}}1=\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\leq\|f\|^{2}_{\infty}\,.

If also
h_{f,f}^{-1}
were a CCP function, then
\widetilde{S}:=h_{f,f}^{-1}[S]\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
(due to
(
6.4.49
)). Hence,

|{\text{tr}}(A^{\top}S)|=|{\text{tr}}(A^{\top}h_{f,f}[\widetilde{S}])|\leq\|f\|^{2}_{\infty}\,\|A\|_{\infty,1}\text{ for all }A\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{R}),

implying that
K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\|f\|^{2}_{\infty}
. If we join the latter
observation and
Theorem 5.2.3
-
(v)
, we obtain another
interesting fact (which should be carefully compared with

Corollary
6.2.8
):

Remark 6.4.13
.

Let
k,m,n\in\mathbb{R}
and
h=h_{f,f}
be CCP for some
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. If
\|f\|_{\infty}<\sqrt{K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}}
, then the
inverse function
h_{f,f}^{-1}
is not CCP.

Next, we are going to summarise the remarkable properties of

odd CCP functions
which we found so far and shed some light on an
additional, quite surprising estimate for odd CCP functions
h_{f,f}
which
emerges if we assume in addition that
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
is

bounded
(a.s.); i.e., if
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
.
In particular, if we combine
Theorem 6.2.6
-
(ii)
,

(v)
and
Theorem 6.4.9
-
(ii)
in this
case, we recover Grothendieck’s upper bound as well as Krivine’s upper bound
at once. This follows from
Example
6.4.17
, which is a special
case of our following key result for the real odd CCP case:

Theorem 6.4.14
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that

\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}=1
. Then
\|f\|_{\infty}\geq 1
. Assume
that
f
is odd and
h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
.
Then the following statements hold:

(i)

\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(y)\geq\frac{\pi}{2}\,\frac{1}{\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}}\,y\text{ for all }y\in[0,1]\,.

(ii)

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}}{h_{f,f}^{\text{hyp}}(1)}\,.

In particular, if
\text{sign}\big((h_{f,f}^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)})^{(2n+1)}(0)\big)=(-1)^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq i\,\frac{\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\widetilde{\psi_{f}}(i)}\,,

where
\psi_{f}:=h_{f,f}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}
and
\widetilde{\psi_{f}}:\overline{\mathbb{D}}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}

is defined as in
Lemma
5.1.12
.

(iii)

Let
1\leq c_{\ast}<K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
. If
\|f\|_{\infty}=1
, then
0<h_{f,f}^{\text{hyp}}(c_{\ast})<1
and there is exactly one number
\gamma^{\ast}(f)\in(h_{f,f}^{\text{hyp}}(c_{\ast}),1]
, such that

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}=\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(\gamma^{\ast}(f))\leq\min\Big\{\frac{1}{h_{f,f}^{\text{hyp}}(1)},\,\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)\Big\}\,.

(6.4.60)

If we only assume that
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\setminus\{0\}
, then an
application of
Theorem 6.4.14
to
\frac{1}{\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}}f\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
directly leads to

Corollary 6.4.15
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\setminus\{0\}
. Then
0<r\equiv r_{k}(f):=\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}^{2}<\infty
and
\|f\|_{\infty}\geq\sqrt{r}
. Assume that
f
is odd and
h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
. Then
\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r)>1
, and the following statements hold:

(i)

\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(y)\geq\frac{\pi}{2}\,\frac{1}{\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}}\,y\,\text{ for all }\,y\in[0,r]\,.

(ii)

\displaystyle r\,K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}}{h_{\frac{f}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{f}{\sqrt{r}}}^{\text{hyp}}(1)}\,.

(6.4.61)

In particular, if
\text{sign}\big((h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)})^{(2n+1)}(0)\big)=(-1)^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq i\,\frac{\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\widetilde{\psi_{f}}(i)}\,,

where
\psi_{f}:=h_{f,f}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}

and
\widetilde{\psi_{f}}:\overline{\mathbb{D}}\longrightarrow r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}}
is
defined as in
Lemma
5.1.12
.

(iii)

Let
1\leq c_{\ast}<K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
. If
\|f\|_{\infty}=\sqrt{r}
, then

0<h_{\frac{f}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{f}{\sqrt{r}}}^{\text{hyp}}(c_{\ast})<1
and there
is exactly one number number
\gamma^{\ast}(f)\in(h_{\frac{f}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{f}{\sqrt{r}}}^{\text{hyp}}(c_{\ast}),1]
, such that

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}=\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r\,\gamma^{\ast}(f))\leq\min\Big\{\frac{1}{h_{\frac{f}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{f}{\sqrt{r}}}^{\text{hyp}}(1)},\,\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r)\Big\}\,.

The proof of
Theorem 6.4.14
-
(i)
shows us that
