here we may circumvent the rather strong assumption of
h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}
being completely real analytic on
(-r,r)
at
0
; at least in the
following sense (cf.
Example
6.4.20
as application for
this):

Proposition 6.4.16
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\setminus\{0\}
. Then
0<r\equiv r_{k}:=\|f\|_{\gamma_{k}}^{2}<\infty
and
\|f\|_{\infty}\geq\sqrt{r}
. Assume that
f
is odd,
h_{f,f}^{\prime}(0)>0
and

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|(h_{f,f}^{-1})^{(2n+1)}(0)|}{(2n+1)!}\,(c^{\ast})^{2n+1}=1\,,

(6.4.62)

for some
c^{\ast}\in(0,r]
. Then
h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-c^{\ast},c^{\ast})}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-c^{\ast},c^{\ast}))
, and the following
statements hold:

(i)

\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-c^{\ast},c^{\ast})}\big)_{\text{abs}}(y)\geq\frac{\pi}{2}\,\frac{1}{\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}}\,y\,\text{ for all }\,y\in[0,c^{\ast}]\,\text{ and }\,\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-c^{\ast},c^{\ast})}\big)_{\text{abs}}(c^{\ast})=1\,.

(ii)

\displaystyle h_{f,f}^{-1}[c^{\ast}\,S]\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}\,\text{ for all }\,S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}\,.

(6.4.63)

(iii)

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}}{c^{\ast}}\,.

Example 6.4.17

(
Grothendieck and Krivine
)
.

Once again, we consider the CCP function
\psi:=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin
. Recall
that
\psi=h_{f,f}
, where
f:=\text{sign}\in S_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\cap S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
. Due to (
6.2.29
), it
follows that

\psi^{\text{hyp}}(1)=\frac{2}{\pi}\,\sinh^{-1}(1)=\frac{2}{\pi}\,\ln(1+\sqrt{2}).

Hence,

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:upper_bound_real_odd_unit_sphere_case}}}{{\leq}}\min\Big\{\frac{1}{\psi^{\text{hyp}}(1)},\,\big(\psi^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)\Big\}\leq\frac{\pi}{2\ln(1+\sqrt{2})}(\approx 1.78221)\leq\sinh(\frac{\pi}{2})(\approx 2.30129)

precisely reflects Krivine’s upper bound of

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
as well as Grothendieck’s (larger) upper bound of

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
!

Example 6.4.18
.

Consider the CCP function
\kappa:=\sqrt{3}(2\phi-1)
(cf.

Proposition
6.3.8
). Due to Theorem

6.4.14
, (i), respectively
(
6.4.61
), we obtain the following (weaker)
estimation:

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{\pi}{6\,\ln(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\sqrt{5}))}\cdot\|\kappa\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq\frac{\pi}{2\,\ln(\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\sqrt{5}))}(\approx 3.26425)\,.

We highly recommend the readers to check whether this estimation can be improved,
if more generally the function
\kappa_{\alpha}:=\alpha(2\phi-1)
is considered,
where
0<\alpha<\sqrt{3}
is given (instead of the CCP function
\kappa=\kappa_{\sqrt{3}}
)! Observe also that
\|\kappa\|_{\infty}=\sqrt{3}\not=1=\|\kappa\|_{\gamma_{k}}
.

Example 6.4.19
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}_{3}
and consider the function
\psi:=h_{f_{k},f_{k}}
, introduced in

Proposition
6.3.1
.
Assume that

\psi^{-1}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
.
\psi
then satisfies all assumptions, listed in

Theorem 6.4.14
, and it follows that
0<c^{\ast}_{k}:=h_{f_{k},f_{k}}^{\text{hyp}}(1)<1
satisfies

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{k}{c^{\ast}_{k}}

(6.4.64)

(since
\|f_{k}\|_{\infty}^{2}=k
). However, observe that the sequence

\big(\frac{k}{c^{\ast}_{k}}\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is not bounded and hence cannot
converge (since
0<c_{k}^{\ast}\leq 1
). Moreover, in contrast to the previous
two examples, we do not know whether also
\big(h_{f_{k},f_{k}}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}
can be represented in a closed analytical form; one
of the major open problems in our search for the smallest upper bound of

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
(cf.
Section
9.1
and
Example
7.3.4
,
where the latter includes the approximation of the constant
c_{k}^{\ast}\approx 0.71200
if
k=2
). A straightforward, yet a bit laborious calculation with
fractions, based on the table (
9.1.8
)
(similarly to the special case
k=2
, studied in
Example
7.3.4
),
yields that the Maclaurin series of
h_{f_{k},f_{k}}^{-1}(s)\equiv\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\beta_{2\nu+1}(k)\,s^{2\nu+1}
can e.g. be approximated
by the Taylor polynomial of degree 7 as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}h_{f_{k},f_{k}}^{-1}(s)&=\beta_{1}(k)s+\beta_{3}(k)s^{3}+\beta_{5}(k)s^{5}+\beta_{7}(k)s^{7}+o(|s|^{7})\\
&=\frac{1}{c_{k}^{2}}\,s+\big(-\frac{1}{c_{k}^{6}}\frac{1}{2(k+2)}\,s^{2}\big)\big(s+\frac{3}{4c_{k}^{4}}\,\frac{k-2}{(k+2)(k+4)}\,s^{3}\\
&+\frac{3}{8c_{k}^{8}}\,\frac{9(k+2)^{2}-48(k+2)+64}{(k+2)^{2}\,(k+4)\,(k+6)}\,s^{5}\big)+o(|s|^{7}).\end{split}

(6.4.65)

Observe also that
\beta_{5}(k):=-\frac{3}{8c_{k}^{10}}\,\frac{k-2}{(k+2)^{2}\,(k+4)}=0

if and only if
k=2
and that
\beta_{5}(k)\leq 0
if and only if
k\geq 2
.
Similarly, since the single (local) minimum of the function
\mathbb{R}\ni x\mapsto g(x):=9x^{2}-48x+64
is attained at
x_{\ast}:=\frac{8}{3}
and
g(x_{\ast})=0
,
it follows that
\beta_{7}(k)\leq 0
.

Example 6.4.20
.

Our approach also can be applied to slightly modify the proof of the
strongest result to date, namely that
K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}<\frac{\pi}{2\ln(1+\sqrt{2})}
.
To this end, firstly observe that a simple change
of variables reveals that
for any

f,g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{k})
, the
corresponding generalised function
H_{f\circ\sqrt{2},g\circ\sqrt{2}}
, listed
in
[
23
, Definition 2.1]
, satisfies

\displaystyle H_{f\circ\sqrt{2},g\circ\sqrt{2}}=h_{f,g}\big|_{(-1,1)}

(6.4.66)

(due to (
6.2.21
)). So,
H_{f\circ\sqrt{2},g\circ\sqrt{2}}
is well-defined on
(-1,1)
. The Grothendieck function

\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin
is then generalised in
[
23
]
to the
complex-valued function

\displaystyle F_{p,\eta}:=(1-p)H_{0}+pH_{\eta}\,,

(6.4.67)

where
0\leq p\leq 1
,
0\leq\eta<1
and
H_{\eta}:\mathbb{S}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

is defined as in
[
23
, (41)]
, where
\mathbb{S}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\operatorname{Re}(z)|<1\}
. Independent of any complex analysis a priori, the
construction of
H_{\eta}
, together with (
6.4.66
) implies
that

H_{\eta}\big|_{\mathbb{S}\cap\mathbb{R}}=H_{\eta}\big|_{(-1,1)}=H_{g_{\eta}\circ\sqrt{2},g_{\eta}\circ\sqrt{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Krivine_RS_vs_h_f_g}}}{{=}}h_{g_{\eta},g_{\eta}}\big|_{(-1,1)}\,.

Here, the odd function
g_{\eta}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as

g_{\eta}(x):=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\,f_{\eta}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}x)
, where
the 2-dimensional sign concept
f_{\eta}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\longrightarrow\{-1,1\}

satisfies
[
23
, (40)]
. Consequently, the function

\frac{2}{\pi}\,H_{\eta}\big|_{(-1,1)}
actually is a restriction of the
well-defined odd - and hence invertible - CCP function
\frac{2}{\pi}\,h_{g_{\eta},g_{\eta}}=h_{\sqrt{2/\pi}\,g_{\eta},\sqrt{2/\pi}\,g_{\eta}}
to the
open interval
(-1,1)
(due to
Theorem 6.4.2
).

Let
\rho\in(-1,1)
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{4}(0,\Sigma_{4}(\rho))
. Since
g_{0}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\,{\text{sign}}(x_{2})
for all
x=(x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we may apply

Proposition
2.2.2
to the partitioned
Gaussian random vector
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})
, and it follows that

H_{0}(\rho)=h_{g_{0},g_{0}}(\rho)=\frac{\pi}{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\tiny{\mathbb{P}_{{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})}}}[\text{sign}(X_{2})\,\text{sign}(Y_{2})]=\arcsin(\rho),

which slightly shortens the proof of
[
23
, Lemma 4.3]
. In particular,
for any
p\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi}\,F_{p,\eta}\big|_{(-1,1)}=\psi_{p,\eta}\big|_{(-1,1)}

(6.4.68)

emerges as a restriction of the odd and hence
strictly increasing,
homeomorphic
CCP function
\psi_{p,\eta}:=(1-p)\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin+p\,\frac{2}{\pi}\,h_{g_{\eta},g_{\eta}}
on
(-1,1)
(due to

Theorem 5.2.3
and
Theorem 6.2.6
-
(iii)
).
Observe that
\psi_{p,\eta}^{\prime}(0)\geq(1-p)\frac{2}{\pi}
, implying
that
\psi_{p,\eta}^{-1}\big|_{(-1,1)}\in C^{\omega}((-1,1))
if
p<1

(due to
Theorem 6.2.6
-
(iv)
). Because of the non-trivial
result
[
23
, Theorem 5.1]
(including its technically demanding proof)
it follows the existence of
(p_{0},\eta_{0})\in(0,1)\times(0,1)
and

c^{\ast}\in(\frac{2}{\pi}\ln(1+\sqrt{2}),\frac{9}{5\pi})
, such that

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|(\psi_{p_{0},\eta_{0}}^{-1})^{(2n+1)}(0)|}{(2n+1)!}\,(c^{\ast})^{2n+1}=1\,.

(Given the outcome of
[
23
, Theorem 5.1]
, we only have to set

c^{\ast}:=\frac{2\gamma}{\pi}
, where
\ln(1+\sqrt{2})<\gamma<\frac{9}{10}

satisfies
[
23
, (63)]
.) Hence,
\psi_{p_{0},\eta_{0}}^{-1}\big|_{(-c^{\ast},c^{\ast})}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-c^{\ast},c^{\ast}))
. Since in particular
\psi_{p_{0},\eta_{0}}^{-1}\big|_{[-c^{\ast},c^{\ast}]}
is
continuous, we may apply

Proposition
6.4.16
. Consequently,
if
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
, then
S_{0}:=\psi_{p_{0},\eta_{0}}^{-1}[c^{\ast}\,S]\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
(due to
(
6.4.63
)).
Observe that for any
0\leq\eta<1
the CCP function
\frac{2}{\pi}\,h_{g_{\eta},g_{\eta}}=h_{\sqrt{2/\pi}\,g_{\eta},\sqrt{2/\pi}\,g_{\eta}}
actually
originates from the
bounded
function
\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,g_{\eta}\in S_{L^{\infty}}\cap S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{2})}
, such as
\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin=h_{\text{sign},\text{sign}}
. Let
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
be arbitrarily
given. Since
S_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}
, we therefore obtain

\displaystyle|{\text{tr}}(A^{\top}S)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\,|{\text{tr}}(A^{\top}\psi_{p_{0},\eta_{0}}[S_{0}])|=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\,\big|{\text{tr}}(A^{\top}\big((1-p)\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin[S_{0}]+p\,\frac{2}{\pi}\,h_{g_{\eta},g_{\eta}}[S_{0}]\big)\big|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:property_of_quantum_corr_matrices}}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\,((1-p)\|A\|_{\infty,1}+p\,\|A\|_{\infty,1})=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\,\|A\|_{\infty,1}\,,

whence

K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}\leq\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}<\frac{\pi}{2\,\ln(1+\sqrt{2})}\,.

In summary, given the - crucial - result
[
23
, Theorem 5.1]
,
our general framework can be applied here as well, leading to a slightly
modified proof of
[
23
, Theorem 1.1]
.
At this point, we would like to highlight another, very recent and
partially altered proof of
[
23
, Theorem 1.1]
– provided by
Krivine again (cf.
[
96
, Theorem 1]
).

Despite the quite remarkable outcome of
Theorem 6.4.14
,
we should observe that its practical implementation seems to be quite difficult
(at least without sufficiently large computer power). Primarily, as we already
have seen, this is due to the following facts:

(i)

Either we have to know a closed form representation (or at least a “close”
approximation of the Maclaurin series) of
h_{f,f}
,
h_{f,f}^{-1}
and

\big(h_{f,f}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}
if
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is given (such
as is the case for
k=1
and
f:=\text{sign}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1})\cap S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{1})}
), or we have to check whether
h=h_{f,f}
“originates”
from some
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k})}
, if the functions

h
,
h^{-1}
and
\big(h^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}
are known to us.

(ii)

However, already in the one-dimensional case (i.e., for
k=1
) that search
requires rather complex calculation techniques, respectively some very helpful
knowledge about Hermite polynomials. Moreover, if
k
increases, we are confronted
with a “curse of combinatorial dimensionality”, since for any
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

and
n\in\mathbb{N}
it can be easily shown by induction on
k
that the set

C(\nu,k):=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}:|n|=\nu\}
which determines the
structure of
h_{f,f}
(cf.
Theorem 6.2.2
) actually consists
of
\binom{\nu+k-1}{k-1}=\frac{(\nu+k-1)!}{\nu!\,(k-1)!}
elements. In
particular, already
C(\nu,2)
consists of
\nu+1
elements (
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
).
For example, to determine
C(2,11)
explicitly, we would have to know all of
its
66
elements!

We will recognise how deep actually we are confronted with a “curse of
combinatorial dimensionality” if only a Maclaurin series representation
of the function
h_{f,f}
is given to us (cf.
Section
9.1
).

Chapter 7
The complex case: towards extending Haagerup’s approach

7.1
Multivariate complex CCP functions and their relation to the real
case

Next, we are going to transfer the main results in the previous
chapter from the real field
\mathbb{R}
to the complex field
\mathbb{C}
. In order to
achieve this, we have to implement non-trivial structural properties of the
class of
complex
Hermite polynomials (cf. Theorem

7.2.3
and Theorem

7.3.1
).
The complex versions of
Theorem 6.2.2
and

Theorem 6.2.6
also allow a generalisation of the Haagerup
equality by transition from the
\Sigma_{2}(\zeta)
-correlated couple of
two complex one-dimensional signum functions
{\text{sign}}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}
to a
\Sigma_{2k}(\zeta)
-correlated couple of two
(possibly different) arbitrary square-integrable functions
b,c:\mathbb{C}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
can be arbitrarily large
(cf.
Corollary
7.1.4
and
Example
7.3.4
). By
definition (cf.
[
62
, Lemma 3.2. and Proof of Theorem 3.1]
), the
complex
sign
-function is given as

{\text{sign}}(z):=\begin{cases}\frac{z}{|z|}&\text{if }z\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\\
0&\text{if }z=0\end{cases}\,.

We introduce the following helpful symbolic constructions and shortcuts. Fix

k,l\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
b:\mathbb{C}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
c:\mathbb{C}^{l}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be two functions. Put

b{}_{k}\!\otimes_{l}c:=(b\circ P_{k})(c\circ Q_{l}),

where

P_{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\ldots&0&0&0&\ldots&0\\
0&1&\ldots&0&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&1&0&0&\ldots&0\end{pmatrix}=(I_{k}\,\brokenvert\,0)\in\mathbb{M}_{k,k+l}(\mathbb{C})

and

Q_{l}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\ldots&0&1&0&\ldots&0\\
0&0&\ldots&0&0&1&\ldots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&0&0&0&\ldots&1\end{pmatrix}=(0\,\brokenvert\,I_{l})\in\mathbb{M}_{l,k+l}(\mathbb{C}),

implying that

\displaystyle b\,{}_{k}\!\otimes_{l}c({\text{vec}}(z\,\brokenvert\,w))=b(z)c(w)\text{ for all }(z,w)\in\mathbb{C}^{k}\times\mathbb{C}^{l}\,.

(7.1.1)

Given the construction of
b\,{}_{k}\!\otimes_{l}c
we may unambiguously shorten
it simply to
b\otimes c
(and suppress the listing of the dimensions of the
domains of definition of
b
, respectively
c
). Let
d:\mathbb{C}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a given function. Recall the induced functions

r(d):\mathbb{R}^{2k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
s(d):\mathbb{R}^{2k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
defined as
r(d):=\operatorname{Re}(d)\circ\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,J_{2}^{-1}
and

s(d):=\operatorname{Im}(d)\circ\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,J_{2}^{-1}
(cf.
(
2.1.8
)). In order to facilitate reading, we
put
d_{\alpha}(z):=d({\text{sign}}(\alpha)z)
, where
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
and

z\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
(implying that
d_{0}=d(0)
). Consequently, the construction of

b\otimes\overline{c}
implies that

\displaystyle r(b\otimes\overline{c})({\text{vec}}({\text{vec}}(x_{1},x_{2}),{\text{vec}}(y_{1},y_{2})))

\displaystyle=r(b)({\text{vec}}(x_{1},y_{1}))r(c)({\text{vec}}(x_{2},y_{2}))

\displaystyle+s(b)({\text{vec}}(x_{1},y_{1}))s(c)({\text{vec}}(x_{2},y_{2}))

and

\displaystyle s(b\otimes\overline{c})({\text{vec}}({\text{vec}}(x_{1},x_{2}),{\text{vec}}(y_{1},y_{2})))

\displaystyle=s(b)({\text{vec}}(x_{1},y_{1}))r(c)({\text{vec}}(x_{2},y_{2}))

\displaystyle-r(b)({\text{vec}}(x_{1},y_{1}))s(c)({\text{vec}}(x_{2},y_{2}))

for all
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. In other words:

\displaystyle r(b\otimes\overline{c})=(r(b)\otimes r(c))\circ G+(s(b)\otimes s(c))\circ G

(7.1.2)

on
\mathbb{R}^{4k}
, and

\displaystyle s(b\otimes\overline{c})=(s(b)\otimes r(c))\circ G-(r(b)\otimes s(c))\circ G\,,

(7.1.3)

on
\mathbb{R}^{4k}
, where again
G=G^{\top}=G^{-1}\in O(4n)
is the matrix,
introduced in (
1.2.9
). Finally,
d
is odd (respectively, even)
if and only if both,
r(d)
and
s(d)=r(-i\,d)
are odd
(respectively, even).

Lemma 7.1.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho\in[-1,1]
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,

c,d:\mathbb{C}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{4k}(0,\Sigma_{4k}(\rho))
. Then

(i)

r(d_{\alpha})=r(d)\circ R_{2}({\text{sign}}(\alpha)I_{k})
and

r(d_{\theta})=r(d)\circ{\text{sign}}(\theta)I_{2k}
.

(ii)

If
\alpha\not=0
, then
r(d)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2k},\gamma_{2k})

if and only if
r(d_{\alpha})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2k},\gamma_{2k})
. In this case, the
norms coincide:
\|r(d)\|_{\gamma_{2k}}=\|r(d_{\alpha})\|_{\gamma_{2k}}
.

(iii)

If
\alpha\not=0
,
h_{r(d),r(d)}(\rho)=\mathbb{E}[r(d)(\textbf{X})r(d)(\textbf{Y})]=\mathbb{E}[r(d_{\alpha})(\textbf{X})r(d_{\alpha})(\textbf{Y})]=h_{r(d_{\alpha}),r(d_{\alpha})}(\rho)
.

(iv)

h_{r(c_{r}),r(d)}(|r|)=h_{r(c),r(d)}(r)\text{ for all }r\in[-1,1]
.

Fix
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{Z},\textbf{W})\sim{\mathbb{C}}N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\zeta))
,
where
\zeta\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}\setminus\{0\}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

b,c:\mathbb{C}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, such that
b\otimes\overline{c}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{2k},\mathbb{P}_{{\text{vec}}(\textbf{Z},\textbf{W})})
. We put

h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}(\zeta):=h^{\mathbb{C}}(b,c;\zeta):=\mathbb{E}\big[b(\textbf{Z})\overline{c(\textbf{W})}\big]=\overline{h^{\mathbb{C}}_{c,b}(\overline{\zeta})}.

As in the real case (see (
6.2.23
)), the
joint multivariate Gaussian splitting property
(
3.5.56
) of inner products of vectors on
the unit sphere and
Lemma
2.2.3
-
(ii)
imply the
important observation that for any separable
\mathbb{C}
-Hilbert space
H
, for any

m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, and for any
(u,v)\in S_{H}^{m}\times S_{H}^{n}
, we have

\displaystyle h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}[\Gamma_{H}(u,v)]=\mathbb{E}\big[\overline{\textbf{R}_{c}}\,{\textbf{S}_{b}}^{\top}\big]=\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}(\textbf{R}_{c},\textbf{S}_{b})],

(7.1.4)

where the
\mathbb{C}^{m}
-valued random vector
\textbf{R}_{c}
and the
\mathbb{C}^{n}
-valued random
vector
\textbf{S}_{b}
are defined as
(\textbf{R}_{c})_{i}:=c(\textbf{Z}_{u_{i}})

and
(\textbf{S}_{b})_{j}:=b(\textbf{Z}_{v_{j}})
, respectively (
(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]
). Fix
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
and put

H_{f,g}:=h_{f,f}+h_{g,g}.

(
6.2.21
) implies a concrete integral representation of the
function
H_{f,g}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}
, which particularly plays an important role in the
complex case (cf.
Corollary
7.1.4
):

\displaystyle H_{f,g}(\rho)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{k}(1-\rho^{2})^{k/2}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\big\langle\binom{f(x)}{g(x)},\binom{f(y)}{g(y)}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{2}}\exp\big(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}-2\rho\langle x,y\rangle}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\big)\textup{d}^{k}x\,\textup{d}^{k}y

(7.1.5)

for all
\rho\in(-1,1)
.

Theorem 6.2.2
further implies
that
H_{f,g}(\rho)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}a_{\nu}\,\rho^{\nu}
for all
\rho\in[-1,1]
, where

a_{\nu}:=p_{\nu}(f,f)+p_{\nu}(g,g)\geq 0
for all
\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In particular,

H_{f,g}(1)=\|f\|^{2}_{\gamma_{2k}}+\|g\|^{2}_{\gamma_{2k}}
, implying that

H_{f,g}
is real analytic on
(-1,1)
, continuous on
[-1,1]
and absolutely monotonic on

[0,1]
. Moreover,
H_{f,g}
is bounded, and

\displaystyle|H_{f,g}(\rho)|\leq\|f\|^{2}_{\gamma_{2k}}+\|g\|^{2}_{\gamma_{2k}}=H_{f,g}(1)

(7.1.6)

for all
\rho\in[-1,1]
. Hence, if
f\not=0
or
g\not=0
, it follows that

\displaystyle H_{f,g}=H_{f,g}(1)\,\psi_{f,g},

(7.1.7)

where the function
\psi_{f,g}:=\frac{H_{f,g}}{H_{f,g}(1)}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-1,1]
is CCP (due to
Theorem 5.2.3
). In particular,

H_{f,g}\Big|_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
. Thus, if
b\in L^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{k},\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}})
, then (
2.1.11
) implies
that
r(b)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2k},\gamma_{2k})
and
s(b)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2k},\gamma_{2k})
,
and

\displaystyle H_{r(b),s(b)}(1)=\|r(b)\|^{2}_{\gamma_{2k}}+\|s(b)\|^{2}_{\gamma_{2k}}=\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}.

(7.1.8)

If - in addition -
b
is odd and

\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}>0
, then also
r(b)
and
s(b)
are odd functions
(such as
H_{r(b),s(b)}
), satisfying
r(b)\not=0
or
s(b)\not=0
.
Hence, we may apply
Theorem 6.2.6
to the well-defined odd CCP function

\psi_{r(b),s(b)}
, and it follows that
H_{r(b),s(b)}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}},\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}]
is a strictly increasing
homeomorphism, such as the inverse function
(H_{r(b),s(b)})^{-1}:[-\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}},\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}]\longrightarrow[-1,1]
(due
to (
7.1.8
) and (
7.1.7
)). Similarly, if we - further -
assume that
H_{r(b),s(b)}^{\prime}(0)>0
,
Theorem 6.2.6
-
(iv)
shows that
also
(H_{r(b),s(b)})^{-1}
is real analytic on
(-\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}},\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}})
. So, we could apply
Lemma
5.1.12

to the real analytic function
(H_{r(b),s(b)})^{-1}{\big|}_{(-\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}},\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}})}
(if
the assumptions are given) to check the existence of

(H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1})_{\text{abs}}:[-\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2},\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
then (a crucial assumption
in
Theorem 7.3.2
). Equipped with these facts,
we arrive at the complex version of
Theorem 6.2.2
:

Theorem 7.1.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\zeta\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
,
\textbf{L}\sim{\mathbb{C}}N_{k}(0,I_{k})
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{Z},\textbf{W})\sim{\mathbb{C}}N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\zeta))
. Let
b\in L^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{k},\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}})
and
c\in L^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{k},\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}})
.
Then

b\otimes\overline{c}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{k}\times\mathbb{C}^{k},\mathbb{P}_{{\text{vec}}(\textbf{Z},\textbf{W})})
.
If
\zeta=0
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{h^{\mathbb{C}}_{c,b}(0)}&=h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}(0)=\mathbb{E}[b(\textbf{L})]\,\mathbb{E}[\overline{c}(\textbf{L})]\\
&=h_{r(b),r(c)}(0)+h_{s(b),s(c)}(0)+i\,(h_{s(b),r(c)}(0)-h_{r(b),s(c)}(0)).\end{split}

(7.1.9)

If
\zeta\not=0
, then

\displaystyle\overline{h^{\mathbb{C}}_{c,b}(\overline{\zeta})}=h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}(\zeta)=h_{r(b_{\zeta}),r(c)}(|\zeta|)+h_{s(b_{\zeta}),s(c)}(|\zeta|)+i\,(h_{s(b_{\zeta}),r(c)}(|\zeta|)-h_{r(b_{\zeta}),s(c)}(|\zeta|)).

(7.1.10)

In particular,

\displaystyle 0\leq h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b_{\overline{\zeta}},b}(\zeta)=H_{r(b),s(b)}(|\zeta|)\text{ and }h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}(1)=\langle b,c\rangle_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}.

(7.1.11)

h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}:\overline{\mathbb{D}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is bounded and satisfies

\displaystyle|h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}(\zeta)|\leq\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}\,\|c\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}\text{ for all }\zeta\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}.

(7.1.12)

By taking into account that

{\text{sign}}(\zeta)\,|\zeta|^{2\nu+1}=\zeta\cdot\zeta^{\nu}\cdot\overline{\zeta}^{\;\nu}
for all
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
and

\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
Theorem 7.1.2
also leads to a
straightforward generalisation of the Haagerup function
(cf.
[
62
, Proof of Theorem 3.1]
and
Example
7.3.4
).
To this end, we introduce a class of complex-valued functions which could be
viewed as a transfer of the class of all odd real-valued functions to the
complex field and contains the complex signum function

\text{sign}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
as element.

Definition 7.1.3
.

Let
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. A function
b:\mathbb{F}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}

is circularly symmetric if

b(\alpha z)=\alpha b(z)\text{ for all }(\alpha,z)\in S_{\mathbb{F}}\times\mathbb{F}^{k}\,.

The set of all circularly symmetric functions is denoted by
CS_{k}(S_{\mathbb{F}})
.

Definition
7.1.3
obviously implies that

CS_{k}(S_{\mathbb{R}})=CS_{k}(\{-1,1\})
coincides with the set of all odd real
functions from
\mathbb{R}^{k}
to
\mathbb{R}
and that
CS_{k}(S_{\mathbb{C}})=CS_{k}(\mathbb{T})
.
Moreover,
(CS_{k}(S_{\mathbb{F}}),\circ,\text{id})
is a monoid (i.e., a semigroup,
with unit element), where the binary operation
\circ
is given by the
composition of functions.

Corollary 7.1.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\zeta\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
. Let
b,c\in L^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{k},\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}})
. Suppose that
b\in CS_{k}(\mathbb{T})
. Then

(i)

\displaystyle h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}(\zeta)={\text{sign}}(\zeta)(h_{r(b),r(c)}(|\zeta|)+h_{s(b),s(c)}(|\zeta|)+i\,(h_{s(b),r(c)}(|\zeta|)-h_{r(b),s(c)}(|\zeta|)))\,.

(7.1.13)

\displaystyle h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}(\zeta)={\text{sign}}(\zeta)H_{r(b),s(b)}(|\zeta|)=\zeta\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}(p_{2\nu+1}(r(b),r(b))+p_{2\nu+1}(s(b),s(b)))\zeta^{\nu}\,\overline{\zeta}^{\;\nu}\,.

(7.1.14)

In particular, we have:

(i-1)

h_{b,b}^{\mathbb{C}}\big|_{[-1,1]}=H_{r(b),s(b)}=h_{r(b),r(b)}+h_{s(b),s(b)}
and
h_{b,b}^{\mathbb{C}}\big|_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
.

(i-2)

If
|\zeta|=1
, then
h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}(\zeta)=\zeta\,\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}
.

(i-3)

If
\zeta\in\mathbb{D}
and
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(|\zeta|))
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}(\zeta)&=\frac{{\text{sign}}(\zeta)}{(2\pi)^{2k}(1-|\zeta|^{2})^{k}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2k}}\big\langle\binom{r(b)(x)}{s(b)(x)},\binom{r(b)(y)}{s(b)(y)}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{2}}\exp\big(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}-2|\zeta|\,\langle x,y\rangle}{2(1-|\zeta|^{2})}\big)\,\textup{d}^{2k}x\,\textup{d}^{2k}y\\
&={\text{sign}}(\zeta)(\mathbb{E}[r(b)(\textbf{X})r(b)(\textbf{Y})]+\mathbb{E}[s(b)(\textbf{X})s(b)(\textbf{Y})]).\end{split}

(7.1.15)

(ii)

h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}
is bounded, and

\displaystyle|h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}(\zeta)|\leq\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}\text{ for all }\zeta\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}.

(7.1.16)

(iii)

If
b\not=0
, then
\frac{1}{\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}}\,H_{r(b),s(b)}
as well as
\frac{1}{\|b\|^{2}_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}}\,h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}

are CCP functions. Both,
H_{r(b),s(b)}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2},\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}]
and

h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}:\overline{\mathbb{D}}\longrightarrow\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}\overline{\mathbb{D}}
are circularly symmetric
homeomorphisms.
H_{r(b),s(b)}
is strictly increasing and satisfies

H_{r(b),s(b)}((-1,0))=(-\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2},0)
and

H_{r(b),s(b)}((0,1))=(0,\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2})
. Moreover,

h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}(\mathbb{D})=\mathbb{D}
and

\displaystyle(h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b})^{-1}(w)={\text{sign}}(w)H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}(|w|)\text{ for all }w\in\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}\overline{\mathbb{D}}\,.

(7.1.17)

(iv)

h_{r(b),r(b)}^{\prime}(0)>0
or
h_{s(b),s(b)}^{\prime}(0)>0

if and only if
H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}{|}_{(-\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2},\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2})}=(H_{r(b),s(b)}{\big|}_{(-1,1)})^{-1}

is real analytic on
(-\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2},\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2})
.

Remark 7.1.5
.

Already the trivial example
b:=1
,
c:=1
and
\zeta:=i
shows us that the additional
assumption
b\in CS_{k}(\mathbb{T})
in
Corollary
7.1.4
cannot be dropped.

Recall (
2.1.13
), including the
construction of the function
g^{\mathbb{C}}
therein. Due to

Proposition
6.2.10
and
Corollary
7.1.4

we obtain

Remark 7.1.6
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\gamma_{k})
. Then
r(f^{\mathbb{C}})=f\otimes 1
and
s(f^{\mathbb{C}})=0
. Moreover,

h_{f,f}=h_{f\otimes 1,f\otimes 1}=H_{r(f^{\mathbb{C}}),s(f^{\mathbb{C}})}\,.

If in addition
f^{\mathbb{C}}\in CS_{k}(\mathbb{T})
, then
h_{f,f}=h^{\mathbb{C}}_{f^{\mathbb{C}},f^{\mathbb{C}}}{\big|}_{[-1,1]}
.

At this point, it is very useful to recall Theorem

6.4.4
and its proof, where we also
implemented Abel’s theorem on
real
power series. From complex analysis
it is well-known that in general Abel’s theorem on power series in this form
does not hold for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, already the structure of the somewhat
“simpler” complex-valued odd functions
h_{b,c}
(cf.
(
7.1.13
) and (
7.1.14
)) seemingly does not
allow a transfer of
Theorem 6.4.4
-
(ii)

and
Theorem 6.4.4
-
(iii)
to the complex
case (due to Theorem
5.2.6
). However,
since
[
61
, Lemma 1.1.13, statement 2]
also holds also for the complex
field, the proof of
Theorem 6.4.4
can be easily
adapted, as well as the proof of
Corollary
6.4.5
,
so that at least the following versions of a complex hybrid correlation transform
can be stated at once:

Proposition 7.1.7
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<r<\infty
and

M:=\begin{pmatrix}A&S\\
S^{\ast}&B\end{pmatrix}\in M_{m+n}(r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{+}

be positive semidefinite, where any entry of the matrices
A,S
and
B
is an
element of
r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}}
. Let
f,g:(-r,r)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be two
functions, such that
(-r,r)\ni x\mapsto f(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}a_{\nu}x^{\nu}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
and
(-r,r)\ni x\mapsto g(x)=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}b_{\nu}x^{\nu}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
. Let
q:r\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic
function, such that

\displaystyle|c_{\nu}|\leq\sqrt{|a_{\nu}|\,|b_{\nu}|}\text{ for all }\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,,

(7.1.18)

where
c_{\nu}:=\frac{q^{(\nu)}(0)}{\nu!}
.
Then
q_{r}\in W^{+}(\mathbb{D})
, where
q_{r}(\zeta):=q(r\zeta)
for all
\zeta\in\mathbb{D}
.
Put
r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}}\ni z\mapsto\tilde{q}(z):=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}c_{\nu}z^{\nu}
,
r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}}\ni z\mapsto f_{\text{abs}}(z):=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}|a_{\nu}|z^{\nu}
and
r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}}\ni z\mapsto g_{\text{abs}}(z):=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}|b_{\nu}|z^{\nu}
. Then
\widetilde{q_{r}}(\frac{z}{r})=\tilde{q}(z)
for all
z\in r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and

\tilde{q}\big|_{r\,\mathbb{D}}=q
, where
\widetilde{q_{r}}\in A(\mathbb{D})
is the
continuous extension of
q_{r}
. Moreover, the following properties hold:

(i)

\begin{pmatrix}f_{\text{abs}}[A]&\tilde{q}[S]\\
\tilde{q}[S]^{\ast}&g_{\text{abs}}[B]\end{pmatrix}\in M_{m+n}(r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{+}

is positive semidefinite.

(ii)

If
0<c_{\ast}\leq r
is a root of
f_{\text{abs}}-1
, then

\tilde{q}[c_{\ast}\,\Gamma]\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}(\mathbb{C})\text{ for all }\Gamma\in\mathcal{Q}_{m,n}(\mathbb{C})\,.

Thus, an application of
Lemma
5.1.12

immediately leads us to

Corollary 7.1.8
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N},A\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{C})
and

\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}M&S\\
S^{\top}&N\end{pmatrix}\in C(m+n;\mathbb{C})

be an arbitrary complex
(m+n)\times(m+n)
correlation matrix (with block elements

M\in C(m;\mathbb{C}),N\in C(n;\mathbb{C})
and
S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}(\mathbb{C})
).
Let
r>0
and
0\not=\psi\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
. Put
r\,\overline{\mathbb{D}}\ni z\mapsto\tilde{\psi}(z):=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}a_{\nu}z^{\nu}
, where
a_{\nu}:=\frac{\psi^{(\nu)}(0)}{\nu!}
. If
0<c\leq r
, then

\frac{1}{\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{\text{abs}}[c\,M]&\tilde{\psi}[c\,S]\\[5.0pt]
\tilde{\psi}[c\,S^{\ast}]&\psi_{\text{abs}}[c\,N]\end{pmatrix}\in C(m+n;\mathbb{C})

again is a correlation matrix with complex entries. In particular,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)}\widetilde{\psi}[cS]\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}(\mathbb{C})\,\text{ for all }\,S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}(\mathbb{C})\,.

(7.1.19)

7.2
On complex bivariate Hermite polynomials

Our next aim is to reveal in detail that it is possible to transfer
the content of
Theorem 6.4.9
from the real case to
the complex one, while maintaining our constructive proof (including the
intended avoidance of the tensor product language). However, we cannot simply
copy the proof of
Theorem 6.4.9
. Nevertheless,
we are going to unfurl that in fact it is possible to transfer
(
6.4.57
) and (
6.4.58
) from
the real case to the complex one. To this end, we are going to work with a particular
case of the rich class of complex bivariate Hermite polynomials, first considered
by K. Itô while working with complex multiple Wiener integrals (cf.

[
80
]
). Similarly to the real case, we need to verify a convenient
correlation property of a random version of these polynomials, which to the
best of our knowledge have not been published before (see Theorem

7.2.3
below). A detailed introduction
to complex Hermite polynomials (which would exceed the topic of this monograph
by far) can be studied in
[
53
,
79
]
.
Firstly, we have to recall the following general construction:

Definition 7.2.1

(
Complex Hermite polynomial
)
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
. The complex Hermite polynomial
H_{m,n}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is defined as

H_{m,n}(z,w):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m!n!}}\sum_{j=0}^{m\wedge n}(-1)^{j}j!\binom{m}{j}\binom{n}{j}z^{m-j}\,w^{n-j}\,.

Within the scope of our work, we need the particular case of
Itô’s complex Hermite polynomials

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\ni z

\displaystyle\mapsto H_{m,n}\circ\kappa(z):=H_{m,n}(z,\overline{z})

\displaystyle=i^{m+n}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{n}i^{j+k}(-1)^{j+n}\sqrt{\binom{m}{j}\binom{n}{k}}s(j,k)H_{j+k}(\sqrt{2}\operatorname{Re}(z))\,s(m-j,n-k)H_{m-j+n-k}(\sqrt{2}\operatorname{Im}(z)),

where
\kappa(z):={\text{vec}}(z,\overline{z})
and
s(\nu,\mu):=\sqrt{\tfrac{(\nu+\mu)!}{\nu\,!\mu!}}
for all
\nu,\mu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, including
the following statements, which we give without proof (cf.
[
80
,
79
]
).

Theorem 7.2.2
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
. Then

(i)

\{H_{m,n}\circ\kappa:m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
is an orthonormal basis in the complex
Hilbert space
L^{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}})
.

(ii)

The exponential generating function of
\{H_{m,n}\circ\kappa:m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}

is given as

\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}H_{m,n}(z,\overline{z})\frac{u^{m}}{\sqrt{m}!}\frac{v^{n}}{\sqrt{n}!}=\exp(uz+v\overline{z}-uv)\text{ for all }u,v\in\mathbb{C}.

Lemma
2.1.7
-
(ii)
allows us to transfer
(the special case
k=1
of)
Corollary
6.2.11

to the complex case. More precisely, we have

Theorem 7.2.3
.

Let
m,n,\nu,\mu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\zeta\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
. If

{\text{vec}}(Z,W)\sim\mathbb{C}N_{2}(0,\Sigma_{2}(\zeta))
, then

\mathbb{E}\big[H_{m,n}(Z,\overline{Z})\overline{H_{\nu,\mu}(W,\overline{W})}\,\big]=\delta_{m,\nu}\,\delta_{n,\mu}\,\zeta^{m}\,\overline{\zeta}^{\,n}=\delta_{(m,n),(\nu,\mu)}\,\zeta^{m}\overline{\zeta}^{\,n}.

7.3
Upper bounds of
K_{G}^{\mathbb{C}}
and inversion of complex CCP functions

Equipped with the complex Hermite polynomials and Theorem

7.2.3
, it is possible to transfer

Theorem 6.4.1
from
the real field
\mathbb{R}
to the complex field
\mathbb{C}
; at least in the odd case.
We “just” have to construct the mappings
\alpha^{\psi,c}_{1}\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}})}
and
\beta^{\psi,c}_{1}\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}})}
properly.

Theorem 7.3.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<c\leq 1
. Let
0\not=\psi\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
be odd.
Then there exist
\alpha\equiv\alpha_{\psi,c},\beta\equiv\beta_{\psi,c}\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}})}
, which satisfy
the following properties:

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\alpha(Z)]=\mathbb{E}[\beta(Z)]=0
for all
Z\sim{\mathbb{C}}N_{1}(0,1)
.

(ii)

If
c\,\zeta\in\mathbb{D}
, then

{\text{sign}}(\zeta)\psi(c|\zeta|)=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\,h_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathbb{C}}(\zeta)

and

{\text{sign}}(\zeta)\psi_{\text{abs}}(c|\zeta|)=h_{\alpha,\alpha}^{\mathbb{C}}(\zeta)\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)=h_{\beta,\beta}^{\mathbb{C}}(\zeta)\psi_{\text{abs}}(c).

In particular,

\displaystyle\psi(c)=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\,\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}}\,.

(7.3.20)

(iii)

If
c\not=1
and
H
is an arbitrary
\mathbb{C}
-Hilbert
space, then

{\text{sign}}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})\psi(c|\langle u,v\rangle_{H}|)=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\,h_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathbb{C}}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})

and

{\text{sign}}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})\psi_{\text{abs}}(c\,|\langle u,v\rangle_{H}|)=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\,h_{\alpha,\alpha}^{\mathbb{C}}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})=\psi_{\text{abs}}(c)\,h_{\beta,\beta}^{\mathbb{C}}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})

for all
u,v\in S_{H}
.

Recall
Corollary
7.1.4
including the structure of

h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,b}
, strongly built on the odd homeomorphic real CCP function

\frac{1}{\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}}\,H_{r(b),s(b)}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow[-1,1]
. Thus, if we link (
7.1.14
),
(
7.1.17
) and Theorem

7.3.1

(where the latter is applied to the function
\psi:=\big(\frac{1}{\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}}\,H_{r(b),s(b)}\big)^{-1}=H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}(\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}\,\cdot)
), we
are able to prove

Theorem 7.3.2

(
Complex inner product rounding
)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
b\in S_{L^{2}(\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}})}
. If
b\in CS_{k}(\mathbb{T})
and

(H_{r(b),s(b)})^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
, then

\big(H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)>1
, and there exist
\alpha_{b}\in L^{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}})
and
\beta_{b}\in L^{2}(\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}})
, satisfying
0<\langle\alpha_{b},\beta_{b}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}}<1
, such that for all
\mathbb{C}
-Hilbert spaces
H
and
u,v\in S_{H}
the following properties are satisfied:

(i)

\displaystyle\langle u,v\rangle_{H}=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}h_{b,b}^{\mathbb{C}}(\zeta_{u,v}(b)),

(7.3.21)

where
0<c^{\ast}\equiv c^{\ast}(b):=H_{r(b),s(b)}^{\text{hyp}}(1)<1
and

\zeta_{u,v}(b):=h_{\alpha_{b},\beta_{b}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\langle u,v\rangle_{H})\in\mathbb{D}
.

(ii)

c^{\ast}=H_{r(b),s(b)}(\langle\alpha_{b},\beta_{b}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}})=h_{b,b}^{\mathbb{C}}(\langle\alpha_{b},\beta_{b}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}}).

(iii)

If
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(|\zeta_{u,v}(b)|))
, then

\displaystyle c^{\ast}\,\langle u,v\rangle_{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\text{sign}}(\zeta_{u,v})}{(2\pi)^{2k}(1-|\zeta_{u,v}|^{2})^{k}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2k}}\big\langle\binom{r(b)(x)}{s(b)(x)},\binom{r(b)(y)}{s(b)(y)}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}_{2}^{2}}\exp\big(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}-2|\zeta_{u,v}(b)|\,\langle x,y\rangle}{2(1-|\zeta_{u,v}(b)|^{2})}\big)\textup{d}^{2k}x\,\textup{d}^{2k}y

\displaystyle={\text{sign}}(\zeta_{u,v})\big(\mathbb{E}[r(b)(\textbf{X})r(b)(\textbf{Y})]+\mathbb{E}[s(b)(\textbf{X})s(b)(\textbf{Y})]\big),

(iv)

If
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(z,w)\in S_{H}^{m}\times S_{H}^{n}
, then there exist
m+n

\mathbb{C}^{k}
-valued random vectors
\textbf{Z}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{Z}_{m},\textbf{W}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{W}_{n}
, such that
{\text{vec}}(\textbf{Z}_{i},\textbf{W}_{j})\sim{\mathbb{C}}N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\zeta_{ij}(b)))
for all
(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]
, and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{H}(z,w)=\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\,\mathbb{E}[\overline{{\textbf{P}}_{b}}{\textbf{Q}}_{b}^{\top}]\,,

(7.3.22)

where
({\textbf{P}}_{b})_{i}:=b(\textbf{Z}_{i})
,

({\textbf{Q}}_{b})_{j}:=b(\textbf{W}_{j})
and

\zeta_{ij}(b):=h_{\alpha_{b},\beta_{b}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\langle z_{i},w_{j}\rangle_{H})\in\mathbb{D}
,

(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]
.

Our next result shows that in fact also
Theorem 6.4.14
,
respectively
Corollary
6.4.15
can be
transferred to the complex case:

Theorem 7.3.3
.

Let
k,m,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
b,c\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{k})
. Then
0\leq r\equiv r_{k}(b):=\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}^{2}<\infty
and
\|b\|_{\infty}\geq\sqrt{r}
. Moreover, the following statements hold:

(i)

|{\text{tr}}(A^{\ast}h^{\mathbb{C}}_{b,c}[S])|\leq\|b\|_{\infty}\,\|c\|_{\infty}\,\|A\|^{\mathbb{C}}_{\infty,1}\text{ for all }A\in\mathbb{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{C})\text{ and }S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{m,n}(\mathbb{C}).

(ii)

Assume that
b\in CS_{k}(\mathbb{T})\setminus\{0\}
and

H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-r,r))
. Put

c^{\ast}\equiv c^{\ast}(b):=H_{\frac{r(b)}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{s(b)}{\sqrt{r}}}^{\text{hyp}}(1)
. Then
c^{\ast}\in(0,1)
, and the
following properties are satisfied:

(ii-1)

\displaystyle r\,K_{G}^{\mathbb{C}}\leq\frac{\|b\|_{\infty}^{2}}{c^{\ast}}\,.

(7.3.23)

In particular, if
\text{sign}\big((H_{\frac{r(b)}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{s(b)}{\sqrt{r}}}^{-1}{|}_{(-1,1)})^{(2n+1)}(0)\big)=(-1)^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

r\,K_{G}^{\mathbb{C}}\leq i\,\frac{\|b\|_{\infty}^{2}}{F_{b}(i)}\,,

where
F_{b}:=

\mathrel{\overset{\scalebox{4.2}[1]{$\sim$}}{\phantom{\psi{|}_{(-1,1)}}}}

H_{\frac{r(b)}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{s(b)}{\sqrt{r}}}{|}_{(-1,1)}

:\overline{\mathbb{D}}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}
is defined as in

Lemma
5.1.12
.

(ii-2)

Let
1\leq\kappa_{\ast}<K_{G}^{\mathbb{C}}
. If
\|b\|_{\infty}=\sqrt{r}
, then

0<H_{\frac{r(b)}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{s(b)}{\sqrt{r}}}^{\text{hyp}}(\kappa_{\ast})<1
and there is exactly one number number
\gamma^{\ast}\equiv\gamma^{\ast}(b)\in(H_{\frac{r(b)}{\sqrt{r}},\frac{s(b)}{\sqrt{r}}}^{\text{hyp}}(\kappa_{\ast}),1]
,
such that

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{C}}=\big(H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r\,\gamma^{\ast})\leq\min\big\{\frac{1}{c^{\ast}},\,\big(H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-r,r)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(r)\big\}\,.

(7.3.24)

Recall again from
Theorem 5.2.6

that in general complex CCP functions need not be analytic. Due to this fact and
the structure of the complex-valued (circularly symmetric) functions
h_{b,b}
,
which is built on absolute values and signs of complex numbers (cf.

Corollary
7.1.4
, including (
7.1.14
)), it
seems that
Proposition
6.4.16

cannot be easily transferred to the complex case (if at all).

Example 7.3.4

(
Haagerup function
)
.

Both,
Corollary
7.1.4
and
Proposition
6.3.1

enable us to recover quickly a direct power series representation of Haagerup’s
CCP function
h_{b,b}:\mathbb{D}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}
, where
b:={\text{sign}}
.
(Retranslated into the terminology of Haagerup,
h_{b,b}=\Phi
and

H_{r(b),s(b)}=\varphi
(cf.
[
62
, Lemma 3.5 and Theorem 3.1]
)).
To this end, let
0\not=\zeta\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}
and
\rho:=|\zeta|
.
As shown on
[
62
, p. 200]
, the function
h_{b,b}
can then be written in
terms of
{\text{sign}}(\zeta)
and the two complete elliptic integrals
E(\rho)

and
K(\rho)
. However, in our approach (by which Haagerup’s CCP function is
obtained as a special case), we don’t have to work with elliptic integration.
Firstly note that

r(b)(x_{1},x_{2})=r(b)(x)=\frac{x_{1}}{\|x\|_{2}}\text{ and }s(b)(x)=r(b)(x_{2},x_{1})=\frac{x_{2}}{\|x\|_{2}}

for all
x={\text{vec}}(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{\{0\}}
. Thus, if
\tau\in[-1,1]
and

{\text{vec}}(\textbf{X},\textbf{Y})\sim N_{2k}(0,\Sigma_{2k}(\tau))
,
k:=2
are given, then

\displaystyle H_{r(b),s(b)}(\tau)

\displaystyle=h_{r(b),r(b)}(\tau)+h_{s(b),s(b)}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[r(b)(\textbf{X})r(b)(\textbf{Y})]+\mathbb{E}[s(b)(\textbf{X})s(b)(\textbf{Y})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[r(b)(X_{1},X_{2})r(b)(Y_{1},Y_{2})]+\mathbb{E}[r(b)(X_{2},X_{1})r(b)(Y_{2},Y_{1})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{X_{1}\,Y_{1}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{2}\,\|\textbf{Y}\|_{2}}\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{X_{2}\,Y_{2}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{2}\,\|\textbf{Y}\|_{2}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\frac{\textbf{X}}{\|\textbf{Y}\|_{2}},\frac{\textbf{Y}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{2}}\big\rangle\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{4}\,\tau\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},2;\tau^{2}\big)=h_{f_{2},f_{2}}(\tau),

whereby the equalities in the last line follow from
Proposition
6.3.1

(applied to
k=2
). An application of
Corollary
7.1.4

(or the original proof of
[
62
, Theorem 3.1]
) therefore implies

{\text{sign}}(\overline{\zeta})h_{b,b}(\zeta)=H_{r(b),s(b)}(\rho)=\frac{\pi}{4}\,\rho\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},2;\rho^{2}\big)=h_{f_{2},f_{2}}(\rho).

Hence,

h_{b,b}(\zeta)={\text{sign}}(\zeta)\,\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\frac{\textbf{X}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{2}},\frac{\textbf{Y}}{\|\textbf{X}\|_{2}}\big\rangle\big]=\frac{\pi}{4}\,\zeta\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},2;|\zeta|^{2}\big)={\text{sign}}(\zeta)\,h_{f_{2},f_{2}}(|\zeta|)\,.

A highly non-trivial part in
[
62
]
consists of a multi-page proof of
the fact that
h_{f_{2},f_{2}}^{-1}=H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
,
implying that
\big(h_{f_{2},f_{2}}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}
is well-defined
(cf.
[
62
, Lemma 2.6]
). The Maclaurin series of
\big(h_{f_{2},f_{2}}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}
can e.g. be approximated by the Taylor polynomial of degree 7 as:

\displaystyle\big(h_{f_{2},f_{2}}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}(s)=\frac{4}{\pi}\,s+\frac{8}{\pi^{3}}\,s^{3}+0\cdot s^{5}+\frac{16}{\pi^{7}}\,s^{7}+o(|s|^{7}).

(7.3.25)

Hence (cf.
Proposition
6.3.1
and
(
7.3.24
)),

\displaystyle\frac{K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}}{\sqrt{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:known_upper_bounds_of_the_complex_GT_constant}}}{{\leq}}K_{G}^{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:upper_bound_complex_odd_bdd_coinciding_norms_case}}}{{\leq}}\min\big\{\frac{1}{h_{f_{2},f_{2}}^{\text{hyp}}(1)},\big(h_{f_{2},f_{2}}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)\big\}

(7.3.26)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt\leq\big(h_{f_{2},f_{2}}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)=\frac{4}{\pi}+\frac{8}{\pi^{3}}+\frac{16}{\pi^{7}}+o(1)\approx 1,53655+o(1).

(7.3.27)

These facts follow from
[
62
]
, respectively
(
6.3.33
) and
(
9.1.8
), where the latter is applied to

\alpha_{\nu}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }\nu\text{ is even}\\
\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{((\nu-2)!!)^{2}}{((\nu-1)!!)^{2}\,(\nu+1)}&\text{if }\nu\text{ is odd}\end{cases}\text{ and }\alpha_{\nu}^{\times}:=\frac{\alpha_{\nu}}{\alpha_{1}}=\frac{\alpha_{\nu}}{c_{2}^{2}}=\frac{4}{\pi}\,\alpha_{\nu}\,.

Already a numerical calculation of the single root
0<c^{\ast}<\frac{\pi}{4}

of the polynomial
s\mapsto\frac{4}{\pi}\,s+\frac{8}{\pi^{3}}\,s^{3}+\frac{16}{\pi^{7}}\,s^{7}-1
leads to the number
\frac{1}{c^{\ast}}\approx 1.40449
.
The latter outcome should now be compared with
the result of Haagerup in
[
62
]
.

Chapter 8
A summary scheme of the main result

To highlight and summarise our approach, it completely suffices to list
in detail the single steps and assumptions in the form of a “flowchart”, possibly
leading to a computer-aided approach regarding the implementation of an
approximation to the lowest upper bound of the Grothendieck constant
K_{G}^{\mathbb{F}}

as a next step.
Very likely, supercomputers are required to perform
these approximations. That (technical) implementation would go however far beyond
the scope of our groundwork; especially since we have no access to equipment of
this type.

Fix
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(SIGN)

Choose a function

0\not=b:\mathbb{F}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}
, which satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

b
is circularly symmetric
(
cf.
Definition
7.1.3
);

(b)

b\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{F}^{k})
.

(CCP)

Consider the function
b^{\circ}_{\mathbb{F}}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
defined as

b^{\circ}\equiv b^{\circ}_{\mathbb{F}}:=\begin{cases}\big(\frac{b}{\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}}}\!\big)^{\mathbb{C}}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}\\
\frac{b}{\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}\,.\end{cases}

Then
\|b^{\circ}\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}}=1
and
1\leq\|b^{\circ}\|_{\infty}=\frac{\|b\|_{\infty}^{\mathbb{F}}}{\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{F}}}}
(
cf.
(
2.1.13
)
). Construct its allocated homeomorphic real CCP function
H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}=h_{r(b^{\circ}),r(b^{\circ})}+h_{s(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}

(
cf.
Corollary
7.1.4
-
(iii)
and

Remark
7.1.6
).

(CRA)

Assume that
{H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}}\in W^{\omega}_{+}((-1,1))
(
cf.

Definition
5.1.13
).

An application of
Lemma
6.2.9
,

Corollary
6.4.15
and Theorem

7.3.3
consequently leads to the
following result which holds for both,
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
simultaneously:

Assume that (SIGN), (CCP) and (CRA) are satisfied. Then

\big(H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)>1
. Put
c^{\ast}_{\mathbb{F}}:=H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}^{\text{hyp}}(1)

(
cf.
Lemma
6.2.9
). Then

0<c^{\ast}_{\mathbb{F}}<1
, and the following statements hold:

(i)

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{F}}\leq\frac{1}{c^{\ast}_{\mathbb{F}}}\,\|b^{\circ}\|_{\infty}^{2}\,.

In particular, if
\text{sign}\big((H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)})^{(2n+1)}(0)\big)=(-1)^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

K_{G}^{\mathbb{F}}\leq i\,\frac{\|b^{\circ}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\widetilde{\psi_{b}}(i)}\,,

where
\psi_{b}:=H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}
and

\widetilde{\psi_{b}}:\overline{\mathbb{D}}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}

is defined as in
Lemma
5.1.12
.

(ii)

Let
1\leq\kappa_{\ast}<K_{G}^{\mathbb{F}}
. If
\|b\|_{\infty}^{\mathbb{F}}=\|b\|_{\gamma_{k}^{\mathbb{F}}}
, then
0<H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}^{\text{hyp}}(\kappa_{\ast})<1
and there is exactly one number
\gamma^{\ast}_{\mathbb{F}}\equiv\gamma^{\ast}_{\mathbb{F}}(b)\in(H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}^{\text{hyp}}(\kappa_{\ast}),1]
,
such that

\displaystyle K_{G}^{\mathbb{F}}=\big(H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(\gamma^{\ast}_{\mathbb{F}})\leq\min\Big\{\frac{1}{c^{\ast}_{\mathbb{F}}},\,\big(H_{r(b^{\circ}),s(b^{\circ})}^{-1}{\big|}_{(-1,1)}\big)_{\text{abs}}(1)\Big\}\,.

Again, we recognise that Maclaurin series representation (or at least
its approximation by the Taylor polynomial of a given degree) and
Maclaurin series inversion of CCP functions play the key role regarding the
search for the lowest upper bound of the Grothendieck constant
K_{G}^{\mathbb{F}}
.
Unfortunately, a closed form representation of the coefficients of the inverse of
a Taylor series runs against a well-known combinatorial complexity issue (due to
the presence of ordinary partial Bell polynomials as building blocks of these
coefficients - cf.
Section
9.1
below for details), which in
general does not allow a closed form representation of these coefficients. The
inverse of the real function factor of the Haagerup function is one such
example. It is given by

[-1,1]\ni\tau\mapsto H_{r(\text{sign}),s(\text{sign})}(\tau)=h_{f_{2},f_{2}}(\tau)=\frac{\pi}{4}\,\tau\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},2;\tau^{2}\big)

in the complex case (cf.
[
62
]
, Remark on page 216 and

Example
7.3.4
), as opposed to the Grothendieck function

[-1,1]\ni\rho\mapsto H_{r(\text{sign}^{\mathbb{C}}),s(\text{sign}^{\mathbb{C}})}(\rho)=h_{\text{sign},\text{sign}}(\rho)=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin(\rho)

in the real case.

Chapter 9
Concluding remarks and open problems

Not very surprisingly, the long-standing, intensive and technically
quite demanding attempts to compute the - still not available - value of the real
and complex Grothendieck constants (an open problem since 1953) leads to further
projects and open problems, such as the following ones; addressed in
particular to researchers who also wish to get a better understanding of the
reasons underlying these topics.

9.1
Open problem 1: Grothendieck constant versus Taylor series
inversion

Only between 2011 and 2013 it was shown that
K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}
is strictly smaller than
Krivine’s upper bound, stating that
K_{G}^{\mathbb{R}}<\frac{\pi}{2\ln(1+\sqrt{2})}

(cf.
[
23
]
and
Example
6.4.20
). Consequently,
in the real case
sign
is not the “optimal” function to choose
(answering a question of H. König to the negative - cf.
[
90
]
). So,
if we wish to reduce the value of the upper bound of the real Grothendieck
constant we have to look for functions
b:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

which are different from
\text{sign}:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\{-1,1\}
.
However, these functions are required to satisfy any of the conditions in the
workflow, listed in
Chapter
8
. In particular, we have to
look for both, the Fourier-Hermite coefficients of the Taylor series
(respectively the approximating Taylor polynomial) of

H_{r(b),s(b)}=h_{r(b),r(b)}+h_{s(b),s(b)}
and the coefficients of the
Taylor series of both, the
inverse
function

H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}
and
\big(H_{r(b),s(b)}^{-1}\big)_{\text{abs}}
.
It is well-known that the latter task increases rapidly in computational
complexity if we want to calculate such Taylor coefficients of a higher degree,
leading to the involvement of highly non-trivial combinatorial aspects,
concretised by the use of partitions of positive integers and partial
exponential Bell polynomials as part of the Taylor coefficients of the inverse
Taylor series (a thorough introduction to this framework including the related
Lagrange-Bürmann inversion formula is given in
[
30
,
88
]
).

To reveal the origin of these difficulties let us focus
on the one-dimensional real case. Let
b\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\gamma_{1})
be given. Assume
that
\alpha_{0}:=h_{b,b}(0)=0
(which is the case if
b
were odd).
First recall from (
6.2.16
) that

h_{b,b}(\rho)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\langle b,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}\,\rho^{n}

for all
\rho\in[-1,1]
, where for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}

H_{n}(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}(-1)^{n}\exp\big(\frac{x^{2}}{2}\big)\,\frac{\textup{d}^{n}}{\textup{d}x^{n}}\exp\big(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\big)

denotes the (probabilistic version of the)
n
-th Hermite polynomial. Put

\alpha_{n}:=\langle b,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}
. If
h_{b,b}^{\prime}(0)=\alpha_{1}>0
,
we know that the real analytic function
h_{b,b}\big|_{(-1,1)}
is invertible
around
0=h_{b,b}(0)
. Its inverse is also expressible as a power series there;
i.e., around
0
,
\big(h_{b,b}\big|_{(-1,1)}\big)^{-1}
is real analytic,
too. Hence, given the assumption (CRA), listed in
Chapter
8
,
it follows that
g_{b}(y):=h_{b,b}^{-1}(y)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\beta_{n}\,y^{n}

for all
y\in[-1,1]
, where
\beta_{1}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{n}&=\frac{1}{n}\,\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{n+k}}(-1)^{k}\,\binom{n-1+k}{k}\,B_{n-1,k}^{\circ}(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\ldots,\alpha_{n-k+1})\\
&=\frac{1}{n\alpha_{1}^{n}}\,\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{k}\,\binom{n-1+k}{k}\,B_{n-1,k}^{\circ}(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}},\frac{\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\alpha_{n-k+1}}{\alpha_{1}})\end{split}

(9.1.1)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{2}
. In this context,

B_{n,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-k+1}):=\sum_{\nu\in P(n,k)}\binom{k}{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n-k+1}}\,\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n-k+1}x_{i}^{\nu_{i}}=\sum_{\nu\in P(n,k)}k!\frac{x^{\nu}}{\nu!}

denotes the ordinary partial Bell polynomial, where the multinomial coefficient

\binom{\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}\nu_{i}}{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n-k+1}}:=\frac{\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}\nu_{i}\big)!}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1-k}{\nu_{i}}!}

represents the number of ways of allocating
\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}\nu_{i}
distinct
objects into
n-k+1
distinct bins, with
\nu_{i}
objects in the
i
’th bin and

P(n,k)
indicates the set of all multi-indices
\nu\equiv(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n+1-k})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n-k+1}
(
k\leq n
) which
satisfy the Diophantine equations

\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-k}\nu_{i}=k\text{ and }\sum_{i=1}^{n+1-k}i\,\nu_{i}=n\,;

i.e., summation is extended over all partitions of the number
n
into
k
positive
(non-zero) integers (cf. e.g.
[
30
,
32
,
104
,
149
]
). Observe that
(dependent on the choice on
n
and
k
, of course) these Diophantine equations
may have an extremely large, if not even an unmanageable set of solutions!
Already that definition implies the well-known and important fact that

\displaystyle B_{n,k}^{\circ}(abx_{1},ab^{2}x_{2},\ldots,ab^{n-k+1}x_{n-k+1})=a^{k}\,b^{n}B_{n,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-k+1}),

(9.1.2)

for all
a,b,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-k+1}\in\mathbb{C}
. If the element
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-k+1})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n-k+1}
consists of at most 2 non-zero elements,
x_{i_{1}}
and
x_{i_{2}}
, say
(
i_{1}<i_{2}
), we only have to sum over the set of all
\nu\in P(n,k)
, such that
\nu_{i}=0

for all
i\notin\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
(since
0^{0}=1
). In this case, the Diophantine equations
reduce to a simple 2-dimensional linear equation system. The latter has a solution

(\nu_{i_{1}},\nu_{i_{2}})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
if and only if

\displaystyle i_{1}\leq\frac{n}{k}\leq i_{2}\text{ and }i_{2}-i_{1}\text{ divides both, }ki_{2}-n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\text{ and }n-ki_{1}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,.

(9.1.3)

The solution
(\nu_{i_{1}},\nu_{i_{2}})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
is then unique and given as

\nu_{i_{1}}=\frac{ki_{2}-n}{i_{2}-i_{1}}\text{ and }\nu_{i_{2}}=\frac{n-ki_{1}}{i_{2}-i_{1}}\,.

Hence, if (
9.1.3
) is satisfied, we immediately recognise that

B_{n,k}^{\circ}(0,0,\ldots,0,x_{i_{1}},0,0,\ldots,0,x_{i_{2}},0,0,\ldots,0)=\binom{k}{\nu_{i_{1}}}x_{i_{1}}^{\nu_{i_{1}}}x_{i_{2}}^{\nu_{i_{2}}}\,.

In particular, if
k\leq n\leq 2k
, we reobtain the well-known special case

B_{n,k}^{\circ}(x,y,0,\ldots 0)=\binom{k}{2k-n}x^{2k-n}y^{n-k}\text{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{C}\,.

In the literature, one frequently finds the so-called
exponential partial Bell
polynomials
B_{n,k}
, characterised as (cf. e.g.
[
30
, Remark on page 136]
)

\displaystyle B_{n,k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-k+1}):=\frac{n!}{k!}B_{n,k}^{\circ}\big(\frac{x_{1}}{1!},\frac{x_{2}}{2!},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-k+1}}{(n-k+1)!}\big),

(9.1.4)

implying that any result about
B_{n,k}
can be directly converted into a result
about
B_{n,k}^{\circ}
and conversely. For example, if
n>k
, then

[
30
, formula (3l)]
transforms very pleasantly and reiteratively into

\displaystyle B_{n,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots x_{n-k+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=k-\alpha(n,k)}^{k-1}\binom{k}{l}x_{1}^{l}\,B_{n-k,k-l}^{\circ}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-2k+l+1})

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{k-\alpha(n,k)}\sum_{l=0}^{\alpha(n,k)-1}\binom{k}{\alpha(n,k)-l}\,x_{1}^{l}\,B_{n-k,\alpha(n,k)-l}^{\circ}(x_{2},\ldots,x_{(n-2k)^{+}+l+2})

for all
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-k+1}\in\mathbb{C}
, where
\alpha(n,k):=\min\{n-k,k\}
.
Another application of (
9.1.4
) implies
the well-known fact that (
9.1.1
)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\big(h_{b,b}^{-1}\big)^{(n)}(0)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\delta_{1}^{n+k}}(-1)^{k}\,\frac{(n-1+k)!}{(n-1)!}\,B_{n-1,k}(\delta_{2},\delta_{3},\ldots,\delta_{n-k+1}),

(9.1.5)

where
\delta_{l}:=\tfrac{h_{b,b}^{(l)}(0)}{l},l\in[n-k+1]
(cf.

[
30
, Theorem E on p. 150]
).

Regarding an explicit recursive construction of these polynomials in
full generality, yet without having to know the sets
P(n,k)
beforehand, we
recall the important fact that any ordinary partial Bell polynomial

B_{n,k}^{\circ}
actually arises as a (kind of) discrete convolution of two
ordinary partial Bell polynomial series. More precisely, we have:

Lemma 9.1.1
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{k}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-k+1}\in\mathbb{C}
. Then
the following equalities are satisfied:

(i)

B_{m,0}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m+1})=\delta_{m0}
and

\displaystyle B_{n,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-k+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=k-1}^{n-1}x_{n-i}\,B_{i,k-1}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-k+2})=\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}x_{i}\,B_{n-i,k-1}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-k+2-i}).

(ii)

\displaystyle nB_{n,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-k+1})

\displaystyle=k\sum_{i=k-1}^{n-1}(n-i)x_{n-i}\,B_{i,k-1}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{i-k+2})

\displaystyle=k\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}ix_{i}\,B_{n-i,k-1}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-k+2-i}).

Lemma
9.1.1
obviously implies
the following multiple-sum representation of the ordinary partial Bell
polynomials:

\displaystyle B_{n,k+1}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1}=k}^{n-1}\sum_{i_{2}=k-1}^{i_{1}-1}\ldots\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{i_{k-1}-1}x_{n-i_{1}}\,\prod\limits_{\nu=2}^{k}x_{i_{\nu-1}-i_{\nu}}\,x_{i_{k}}

(9.1.6)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{k+1}
and
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-k}\in\mathbb{C}
.
For the convenience of the readers, we list a few examples of ordinary partial Bell
polynomials that can be displayed in closed form. For review, we
refer to the widely comprehensive table of these polynomials on page 309 of

[
30
]
(enumerating all polynomials
B_{n,m}^{\circ}
for which
10\geq n\geq m\geq 1
). To this end, fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k+1}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Then

(i)

B_{0,0}(x_{1})=1
and
B_{k,0}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k+1})=0
.

(ii)

B_{k,1}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=x_{k}
and
B_{k,k}(x_{1})=x_{1}^{k}
.

(iii)

B_{k+1,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2})=k\,x_{1}^{k-1}\,x_{2}
.

(iv)

B_{k+2,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\begin{pmatrix}k\\
2\end{pmatrix}\,x_{1}^{k-2}\,x_{2}^{2}+k\,x_{1}^{k-1}\,x_{3}
if
k\geq 2
.

(v)

B_{k+3,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=\begin{pmatrix}k\\
3\end{pmatrix}\,x_{1}^{k-3}\,x_{2}^{3}+k(k-1)\,x_{1}^{k-2}\,x_{2}\,x_{3}+k\,x_{1}^{k-1}\,x_{4}
if
k\geq 3
.

(vi)

B_{k+4,k}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{5})=\begin{pmatrix}k\\
4\end{pmatrix}\,x_{1}^{k-4}\,x_{2}^{4}+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
3\end{pmatrix}\,x_{1}^{k-3}(3\,x_{2}^{2}\,x_{3})+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
2\end{pmatrix}\,x_{1}^{k-2}(x_{3}^{2}+2\,x_{2}\,x_{4})+k\,x_{1}^{k-1}\,x_{5}
if
k\geq 4
.

Moreover, we have

B_{k,2}^{\circ}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1})=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}x_{i}\,x_{k-i}\,\text{ if }\,k\geq 2\,.

Since the Taylor series of the inverse of the “standardised”
Taylor series
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{n}^{\times}\,\rho^{n}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\,h_{b,b}(\rho)
of the function
h_{b,b}
obviously is given by
\big(\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\,h_{b,b}\big)^{-1}(y)=h_{b,b}^{-1}(\alpha_{1}\,y)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\beta_{n}\,\alpha_{1}^{n})y^{n}
for all
y\in[-1,1]
, where
\alpha_{n}^{\times}:=\frac{\alpha_{n}}{\alpha_{1}}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
), it follows that the
n
-th Taylor
series coefficient
\beta_{n}^{\times}
of the Taylor series of
\big(\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\,h_{b,b}\big)^{-1}
is given by
\beta_{n}^{\times}=\beta_{n}\,\alpha_{1}^{n}
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\beta_{n}\,\alpha_{1}^{n}=\beta_{n}^{\times}=\frac{1}{n}\,\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\,(-1)^{k}\binom{n-1+k}{k}\,B_{n-1,k}^{\circ}(\alpha_{2}^{\times},\alpha_{3}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n-k+1}^{\times})

(9.1.7)

(due to (
9.1.1
)).
In the odd case, i.e., if in addition
\alpha_{2n}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the intrinsic combinatorical complexity of
(
9.1.1
) can be even
further reduced, possibly allowing a non-negligible saving of computing time
(see
Corollary
9.1.6
).

We explicitly list
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\beta_{4},\beta_{5},\beta_{6}
and
\beta_{7}
in full generality. To this end, as discussed above,
if
\alpha_{1}\not=0
, we have to consider the Taylor coefficents

\alpha_{n}^{\times}:=\frac{\alpha_{n}}{\alpha_{1}},n\in\mathbb{N}
of the
“standardised” Taylor series
\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\,h_{b,b}
. Note again that

\alpha_{1}^{\times}=1
.
(
9.1.1
) therefore
implies that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{1}\,\alpha_{1}&=1=\alpha_{1}^{\times}\\
\beta_{2}\,\alpha_{1}^{2}&=-\alpha_{2}^{\times}\\
\beta_{3}\,\alpha_{1}^{3}&=-\alpha_{3}^{\times}+2(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{2}\\
\beta_{4}\,\alpha_{1}^{4}&=-\alpha_{4}^{\times}+5\alpha_{2}^{\times}\alpha_{3}^{\times}-5(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{3}\\
\beta_{5}\,\alpha_{1}^{5}&=-\alpha_{5}^{\times}+6\alpha_{2}^{\times}\alpha_{4}^{\times}+3(\alpha_{3}^{\times})^{2}-21(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{2}\alpha_{3}^{\times}+14(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{4}\\
\beta_{6}\,\alpha_{1}^{6}&=-\alpha_{6}^{\times}+7\alpha_{2}^{\times}\alpha_{5}^{\times}+7\alpha_{3}^{\times}\alpha_{4}^{\times}-28\alpha_{2}^{\times}(\alpha_{3}^{\times})^{2}-28(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{2}\alpha_{4}^{\times}+84(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{3}\alpha_{3}^{\times}-42(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{5}\\
\beta_{7}\,\alpha_{1}^{7}&=-\alpha_{7}^{\times}+8\alpha_{2}^{\times}\alpha_{6}^{\times}+8\alpha_{3}^{\times}\alpha_{5}^{\times}+4(\alpha_{4}^{\times})^{2}-36(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{2}\alpha_{5}^{\times}-72\alpha_{2}^{\times}\alpha_{3}^{\times}\alpha_{4}^{\times}-12(\alpha_{3}^{\times})^{3}+120(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{3}\alpha_{4}^{\times}\\
&\hskip 12.80365pt+180(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{2}(\alpha_{3}^{\times})^{2}-330(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{4}\alpha_{3}^{\times}+132(\alpha_{2}^{\times})^{6}\,.\end{split}

(9.1.8)

In fact, if we make use of the key result, listed in
[
158
]
, paired with
the general Theorem
[
27
, p. 222]
, we are able to present a (purely linear
algebraic and algorithmic) representation of each coefficient

\beta_{n}
, which avoids an explicit use of ordinary partial Bell polynomials (where no closed
form seems to be available). To the best of our knowledge, in this context,
that representation had not been published before. Instead of ordinary partial Bell
polynomials, we have to calculate determinants of leading principal submatrices. Of course,
the computational complexity induced by the increasing size of partial Bell
polynomials transforms into the rapidly increasing computing time, induced by the increasing
size of the determinants including the need to sum proper parts of determinants of

different
size. However, that summation is a recurrence relation (see
(
9.1.11
) and the instructive
Example
9.1.3
).

Firstly, if
\alpha_{1}\not=0
, an enhancement of
[
158
]

reveals the following explicit representation of each coefficient
\beta_{n}

(
n\in\mathbb{N}_{2}
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{n}&=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n!\,\alpha_{1}^{n}}\,\det(A_{n}\ast T_{n}(1,\alpha_{2}^{\times},\alpha_{3}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{\times}))\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n!\,\alpha_{1}^{2n-1}}\,\det(A_{n}\ast T_{n}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}))\\
&=\frac{1}{n!\,\alpha_{1}^{2n-1}}\,\det(-(A_{n}\ast T_{n}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}))),\end{split}

(9.1.9)

where
\ast
again denotes the Hadamard product and the matrices
A_{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R})
and
T_{n}\equiv T_{n}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{M}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R})
(
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
) are respectively defined as

A_{n}:=\begin{pmatrix}n&1&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
2n&n+1&2&0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
3n&2n+1&n+2&3&0&\cdots&0&0\\
4n&3n+1&2n+2&n+3&4&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
(n-3)n&(n-4)n+1&(n-5)n+2&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&n-3&0\\
(n-2)n&(n-3)n+1&(n-4)n+2&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&n+(n-3)&n-2\\
(n-1)n&(n-2)n+1&(n-3)n+2&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&2n+(n-3)&n+(n-2)\end{pmatrix}

and

T_{n}\equiv T_{n}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}):=\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}&x_{1}&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
x_{3}&x_{2}&x_{1}&0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
x_{4}&x_{3}&x_{2}&x_{1}&0&\cdots&0&0\\
x_{5}&x_{4}&x_{3}&x_{2}&x_{1}&\ddots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{n-2}&x_{n-3}&\cdots&\cdots&x_{3}&x_{2}&x_{1}&0\\
x_{n-1}&x_{n-2}&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&x_{3}&x_{2}&x_{1}\\
x_{n}&x_{n-1}&x_{n-2}&\cdots&\cdots&x_{4}&x_{3}&x_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

Obviously, the Toeplitz matrix
T_{n}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
is well-defined
for any
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
. Observe that the appearance of the rather
uncommon factor
\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2n-1}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{n}}\cdot\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{n-1}}
in (
9.1.9
) actually
originates from the simple, yet important, transformation

\displaystyle T_{n}\equiv T_{n}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n})=\alpha_{1}\,T_{n}(1,\alpha_{2}^{\times},\alpha_{3}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{\times}).

(9.1.10)

More precisely, if
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(i,j)\in[n-1]\times[n-1]
, we have:

(A_{n})_{ij}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }j\geq i+2\\
i&\text{if }j=i+1\\
(i-j+1)n+j-1&\text{if }j\leq i\end{cases}\text{ and }(T_{n})_{ij}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }j\geq i+2\\
\alpha_{1}&\text{if }j=i+1\\
x_{i-j+2}&\text{if }j\leq i\end{cases}.

Comparing (
9.1.1
) and
(
9.1.9
), it follows that for all
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
and

\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{C}

\det((A_{n}\ast T_{n}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})))=\alpha_{1}^{n-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n-1+k}\,\frac{(n-1+k)!}{k!}\,B_{n-1,k}^{\circ}(\alpha_{2}^{\times},\alpha_{3}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n-k+1}^{\times}).

Hence,

\det((A_{n}\ast T_{n}(1,\alpha_{2}^{\times},\alpha_{3}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{\times})))=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n-1+k}\,\frac{(n-1+k)!}{k!}\,B_{n-1,k}^{\circ}(\alpha_{2}^{\times},\alpha_{3}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n-k+1}^{\times}).

Remark 9.1.2

(
Connection to Apostol’s approach in
[
8
]
)
.

In fact,
it can be shown that

\det\big(-\big(A_{n}\ast T_{n}\big(x_{1},\frac{x_{2}}{2!},\frac{x_{3}}{3!},\ldots,\frac{x_{n}}{n!}\big)\big)\big)=P_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\text{ for all }x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{C}

precisely coincides with the function
P_{n}
, introduced in
[
8
]
(due to
the convolution representation and the partial derivative structure of ordinary
partial Bell polynomials)! In particular,

\displaystyle P_{n}(1,x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{k}\,\frac{(n-1+k)!}{k!}\,B_{n-1,k}^{\circ}\big(\frac{x_{2}}{2!},\frac{x_{3}}{3!},\ldots,\frac{x_{n-k+1}}{(n-k+1)!}\big)

\displaystyle=\det\big(-\big(A_{n}\ast T_{n}\big(1,\frac{x_{2}}{2!},\frac{x_{3}}{3!},\ldots,\frac{x_{n}}{n!}\big)\big)\big).

Due to (
9.1.10
), it follows that

A_{n}\ast T_{n}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})=\alpha_{1}(A_{n}\ast T_{n}(1,\alpha_{2}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{\times}))=\alpha_{1}B_{n}[n-1](\alpha_{2}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{\times}),

where
for any
p\in[n-1]
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{p}\in\mathbb{C}
, the matrix
B_{n}[p]\equiv B_{n}[p](x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})\in\mathbb{M}_{p}(\mathbb{C})
is defined as

B_{n}[p]:=\begin{pmatrix}nx_{1}&1&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
2nx_{2}&(n+1)x_{1}&2&0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
3nx_{3}&(2n+1)x_{2}&(n+2)x_{1}&3&0&\cdots&0&0\\
4nx_{4}&(3n+1)x_{3}&(2n+2)x_{2}&(n+3)x_{1}&4&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
(p-2)nx_{p-2}&((p-3)n+1)x_{p-3}&((p-4)n+2)x_{p-4}&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&p-2&0\\
(p-1)nx_{p-1}&((p-2)n+1)x_{p-2}&((p-3)n+2)x_{p-3}&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&(n+(p-2))x_{1}&p-1\\
pnx_{p}&((p-1)n+1)x_{p-1}&((p-2)n+2)x_{p-2}&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&(2n+(p-2))x_{2}&(n+(p-1)x_{1})\end{pmatrix}

B_{n}[p]
therefore denotes the
p
-th leading principal submatrix of the
matrix
B_{n}[n-1]
if
p\in[n-1]
(cf. e.g.
[
73
, 0.7.1]
).
More precisely, if
p\in[n-1]
and
i,j\in[p]
, then:

B_{n}[p](x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{p})_{ij}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }j\geq i+2\\
i&\text{if }j=i+1\\
((i-j+1)n+j-1)x_{i-j+1}&\text{if }j\leq i\,.\end{cases}

Equipped with all
p
leading principal submatrices
B_{n}[1],B_{n}[2],\ldots,B_{n}[p-1]
of
B_{n}[p]
and the “incipient matrix”

B_{n}[0]:=(1)
, we may apply the main (unnumbered) theorem on page 222 of

[
27
]
to the matrix
B_{n}[p](x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})
, and it follows that

\displaystyle\det(B_{n}[p](x_{1},\ldots,x_{p}))

\displaystyle=(p-1)!\sum_{k=1}^{p}(-1)^{p-k}\,\frac{pn-(k-1)(n-1)}{(k-1)!}\,x_{p-k+1}\,\det({B_{n}[k-1]}).

(9.1.11)

Consequently, if
p=n-1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\det(A_{n}\ast T_{n}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}))&=\alpha_{1}^{n-1}\,\det(B_{n}[n-1](\alpha_{2}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{n}^{\times}))\\
&=\alpha_{1}^{n-1}\,(n-2)!\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n-1-k}\,\frac{(n-1)(n-k+1)}{(k-1)!}\,\alpha_{n-k+1}^{\times}\,\det(B_{n}[k-1](\alpha_{2}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{k}^{\times}))\\
&=\alpha_{1}^{n-1}\,(n-1)!\,\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{n-k-1}\,\frac{n-k+1}{(k-1)!}\,\alpha_{n-k+1}^{\times}\,\det(B_{n}[k-1](\alpha_{2}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{k}^{\times})).\end{split}

(9.1.12)

Thus,

\displaystyle\beta_{n}\,\alpha_{1}^{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:matrix_rep_of_beta_ns}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{k}\,\frac{n-k+1}{(k-1)!}\,\alpha_{n-k+1}^{\times}\,\det(B_{n}[k-1](\alpha_{2}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{k}^{\times}))\,.

In particular, if
n=2m+1\in\mathbb{N}_{3}
is odd (
m\in\mathbb{N}
) and

\alpha_{2l}:=0
for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\displaystyle\beta_{n}\,\alpha_{1}^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k=1}}{{k{\text{ odd}}}}}^{n-1}(-1)^{k}\,\frac{n-k+1}{(k-1)!}\,\alpha_{n-k+1}^{\times}\,\det(B_{n}[k-1](0,\alpha_{3}^{\times},0\ldots,0,\alpha_{k}^{\times}))

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{2m+1}^{\times}-\frac{1}{2m+1}\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{2(m-r)+1}{(2r)!}\,\alpha_{2(m-r)+1}^{\times}\,\det(B_{2m+1}[2r](0,\alpha_{3}^{\times},0\ldots,0,\alpha_{2r+1}^{\times})).

Note also that (
9.1.11
) implies that

\displaystyle\det(B_{2m+1}[2r](0,x_{1},0,x_{2},0,\ldots,0,x_{r}))=-2(2r-1)!\,\big((2m+1)r\,x_{r}+\sum_{k=1}^{r-1}p_{k}(r,m)\,x_{r-k}\big)

(9.1.13)

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\in[m]
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in\mathbb{C}
, where

p_{k}(r,m):=\frac{2m(r-k)+r}{(2k)!}\,\det({B_{2m+1}[2k]}(0,x_{1},0\ldots,0,x_{k})\hskip 14.22636pt(k\in[r-1]).

Example 9.1.3
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume for simplification that
\alpha_{1}=1

\alpha_{2l}=0

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\alpha_{n}^{\times}=\alpha_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If

r\in\{1,2\}
, the calculation of
\det({B_{2m+1}[2r]})
is very
straightforward:

\det(B_{2m+1}[2](0,\alpha_{3}))=-2(2m+1)\alpha_{3}=2!(-1)^{1}\binom{2m+1}{1}\alpha_{3}=2!(-1)^{1}\binom{2m+1}{1}B_{1,1}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3}),

and

\displaystyle\det(B_{2m+1}[4](0,\alpha_{3},0,\alpha_{5}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:recursive_constr_of_dets_in_the_odd_case}}}{{=}}-12\,\big(2(2m+1)\alpha_{5}+\frac{2m+2}{2}\,\det({B_{2m+1}[2]})\alpha_{3}\big)

\displaystyle=-12\,\big(2(2m+1)\alpha_{5}+\frac{2m+2}{2}\,\big(-2(2m+1)\alpha_{3}\big)\alpha_{3}\big)

\displaystyle=4!\big(-\binom{2m+1}{1}\alpha_{5}+\binom{2m+2}{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=4!\big((-1)^{1}\binom{2m+1}{1}B_{2,1}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5})+(-1)^{2}\binom{2m+2}{2}B_{2,2}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3})\big).

If
r=3
, a little more calculation effort is required, also triggered by a
significant transformation step:

\displaystyle\det(B_{2m+1}[6](0,\alpha_{3},0,\alpha_{5},0,\alpha_{7}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:recursive_constr_of_dets_in_the_odd_case}}}{{=}}-240\,\big(3(2m+1)\alpha_{7}+\frac{4m+3}{2}\,\det({B_{2m+1}[2]})\alpha_{5}+\frac{2m+3}{4!}\,\det({B_{2m+1}[4]})\alpha_{3}\big)

\displaystyle=-240\,\big(3\binom{2m+1}{1}\alpha_{7}{-(4m+3)\binom{2m+1}{1}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{5}}

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt{+(2m+3)}\big({-\binom{2m+1}{1}\alpha_{5}}+\binom{2m+2}{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}\big){\alpha_{3}}\Big)

\displaystyle=-240\,\Big(3\binom{2m+1}{1}\alpha_{7}{-3\,\binom{2m+2}{2}(2\alpha_{3}\alpha_{5})}+(2m+3)\binom{2m+2}{2}\alpha_{3}^{3}\Big)

\displaystyle=6!\,\Big(-\binom{2m+1}{1}\alpha_{7}+\binom{2m+2}{2}\,(2\alpha_{3}\alpha_{5})-\binom{2m+3}{3}\alpha_{3}^{3}\Big)

\displaystyle=6!\,\Big((-1)^{1}\binom{2m+1}{1}B_{3,1}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7})+(-1)^{2}\,\binom{2m+2}{2}\,B_{3,2}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+(-1)^{3}\,\binom{2m+3}{3}\,B_{3,3}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3})\Big).

In fact, the emerging structure can be kept in the case of
r=4
, since

\displaystyle\det({B_{2m+1}[8]})(0,\alpha_{3},0,\alpha_{5},0,\alpha_{7},0,\alpha_{9})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:recursive_constr_of_dets_in_the_odd_case}}}{{=}}-2\cdot 7!\,\Big(4(2m+1)\alpha_{9}+\frac{6m+4}{2}\,\det({B_{2m+1}[2]})\alpha_{7}

\displaystyle\hskip 31.2982pt+\frac{4m+4}{4!}\,\det({B_{2m+1}[4]})\alpha_{5}+\frac{2m+4}{6!}\,\det({B_{2m+1}[6]})\alpha_{3}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=8!\,\Big(-\binom{2m+1}{1}\alpha_{9}+\binom{2m+2}{2}(2\alpha_{3}\alpha_{7}+\alpha_{5}^{2})-\binom{2m+3}{3}(3\alpha_{3}^{2}\alpha_{5})

\displaystyle\hskip 31.2982pt+\binom{2m+4}{4}\alpha_{3}^{4}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=8!\,\Big((-1)^{1}\,\binom{2m+1}{1}B_{4,1}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7},\alpha_{9})+(-1)^{2}\,\binom{2m+2}{2}B_{4,2}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7})

\displaystyle\hskip 31.2982pt+(-1)^{3}\,\binom{2m+3}{3}B_{4,3}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5})+(-1)^{4}\,\binom{2m+4}{4}B_{4,4}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3})\Big).

A relentless focus on
Example
9.1.3
therefore leads
to a non-obvious simplification of (
9.1.7
) which
reduces the analysis of complex partition sets
P(2m,k)
and related non-trivial ordinary
partial Bell polynomials
B_{2m,k}^{\circ}(0,\alpha_{3},0,\alpha_{5},0,\alpha_{7},0,\ldots)

to that one of partition sets
P(m,l)
and related “fully occupied” ordinary partial Bell
polynomials
B_{m,l}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{7},\ldots)
. In fact, the
following result holds:

Proposition 9.1.4
.

Let
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an arbitrary sequence of complex numbers. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in[m]
.
Then

\det(B_{2m+1}[2r](0,x_{1},0,x_{2},\ldots,0,x_{r}))=(2r)!\sum_{l=1}^{r}(-1)^{l}\binom{2m+l}{l}\,B_{r,l}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{r-l+1}).

Altogether,
Lemma
9.1.1
,

Proposition
9.1.4

and (
), together with our previously mentioned
analysis of the structure of
\beta_{2m+1}
, imply the following two fundamental
results:

Theorem 9.1.5
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{2}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m-1}\in\mathbb{C}
. Then

\displaystyle\hskip 12.80365pt\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{2(m-r)+1}{(2r)!}\,x_{m-r}\,\det\big({B_{2m+1}[2r](0,x_{1},0,x_{2},\ldots,0,x_{r})}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2m)!}\sum_{r=2}^{m}(-1)^{r-1}\,\frac{(2m+r)!}{r!}\,B_{m,r}^{\circ}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{m-r+1}).

Corollary 9.1.6
.

Let
f(\rho)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{2n+1}\,\rho^{2n+1}
be an odd real
analytic function, convergent on
(-r,r)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, where
r>0
denotes
the radius of convergence of
f
. Assume that
f^{\prime}(0)=\alpha_{1}\not=0
, implying that
f
is invertible around
0
. Consider the real analytic odd
inverse function
f^{-1}:V\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
V
is an open
neighbourhood of
f(0)=0
. If
f^{-1}(y)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\beta_{2m+1}\,y^{2m+1}
for all
y\in V
, then
\beta_{1}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}
and

\displaystyle\beta_{2m+1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2m+1}}\Big(\alpha_{2m+1}^{\times}+\frac{1}{2m+1}\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{2(m-r)+1}{(2r)!}\,\alpha_{2(m-r)+1}^{\times}\,\det\big({B_{2m+1}[2r](0,\alpha_{3}^{\times},0,\alpha_{5}^{\times},\ldots,0,\alpha_{2r+1}^{\times})}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2m+1)!\,\alpha_{1}^{2m+1}}\sum_{r=1}^{m}(-1)^{r}\,\frac{(2m+r)!}{r!}\,B_{m,r}^{\circ}(\alpha_{3}^{\times},\alpha_{5}^{\times},\ldots,\alpha_{2(m-r+1)+1}^{\times})

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\alpha_{2\nu+1}^{\times}:=\frac{\alpha_{2\nu+1}}{\alpha_{1}}
(
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
).

As a little, yet illuminating exercise, we recommend the readers to
perform the rather quick calculation of the first 3 Taylor coefficents of the
inverse of the odd function
f:=3\sinh
, say, by applying

Corollary
9.1.6
! The outcome could be
double-checked by means of

https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverse_hyperbolic_functions#Series_expansions
.
Moreover, because of
[
149
, Theorem 2]
the coefficients
\beta_{2m+1}

satisfy the following, interesting recurrence relation:

\displaystyle\beta_{2m+1}\alpha_{1}^{2m+1}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}(\beta_{2r+1}\alpha_{1}^{2r+1})\,B_{2m+1,2r+1}^{\circ}(\alpha_{1}^{\times},0,\alpha_{3}^{\times},0,\alpha_{5}^{\times},\ldots,0,\alpha_{2(m-r)+1}^{\times})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}\beta_{2r+1}\,B_{2m+1,2r+1}^{\circ}(\alpha_{1},0,\alpha_{3},0,\alpha_{5},\ldots,0,\alpha_{2(m-r)+1})

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

In a nutshell, we recognise that already in the one-dimensional case,
at least two hard open problems appear. On the one hand we need to know the
explicit value of the Fourier-Hermite coefficients (cf.

Proposition
6.1.3
)

\sqrt{n!}\,\langle b,H_{n}\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}=\sqrt{n!}\,\mathbb{E}[b(X)H_{n}(X)]=\frac{\textup{d}^{n}}{\textup{d}t^{n}}\mathbb{E}[b(X+t)]\Big|_{t=0}\,,

where
X\sim N_{1}(0,1)
. On the other hand, we have to look for a closed form
expression of the coefficients
\beta_{n}
(if it were available at all), where
the latter involves the complex recursive structure of ordinary partial Bell
polynomials or related determinants. For example (keeping the Haagerup function
in mind - cf.
Example
7.3.4
and

Remark
4.1.1
), our question of the value of

\frac{\pi^{k}}{4^{k}}\,B_{n,k}^{\circ}\Big(\frac{1}{8},\frac{3}{64},\frac{25}{1024},\ldots,\frac{((2(n-k)+1)!!)^{2}}{((2(n-k+1))!!)^{2}\,(n-k+2)}\Big)

very recently lead to an in depth-analysis, published in
[
42
]
. It
appears to us that in general one cannot use proofs by standard induction on

n\in\mathbb{N}
to verify statements about Bell polynomials. The Noetherian Induction
Principle seems to be more appropriate here (as we have seen for example, in the
proof of
Proposition
9.1.4
).
In this context, we would like to draw attention to another recently published
paper, where the authors point to similar difficulties including the formulation
of related - open - problems (cf.
[
101
]
). Moreover, the solved examples
in
[
101
]
show the large combinatorial barriers which we have to resolve
while working with (partial) Bell polynomials.

Keeping these problems and barriers in mind, the following research topics and
problems - which actually do not require any knowledge of the Grothendieck
inequality - arise naturally:

(RP1)

Continue to investigate the structure of partial Bell polynomials;
possibly under inclusion of the use of supercomputers and related
computer algebra systems.

(RP2)

Develop a software package which puts

Corollary
9.1.6
into practice.

(RP3)

Look for an explicit analytic expression for the
inverse

function of the main building block of the Haagerup function; i.e., the inverse
of the strictly increasing odd function

[-1,1]\ni x\mapsto x\,{}_{2}F_{1}(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},2;x^{2})

(if available !), where as usual
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c;\cdot)
denotes the classic
Gaussian hypergeometric function (cf.
Example
7.3.4
). Obviously,
the inverse of
